The habit had no use for nine. The habit's arithmetic stops at *four* — four in, four out, four pause — but the habit's arithmetic *proceeds* from four to *seven* every time it has counted *everything it can count*, the way a register's arithmetic proceeds from the open account to the closed account after the day is over: the habit's arithmetic stops at four but completes at *seven*, and the number *seven*, in the habit's grammar, means *the one who has been counted all the way down to the bottom of the count*, which is to say *the one who has been counted *twice*** — once on the way in, once on the way out — and whose count has been verified, by being checked against itself, the way a mother checks a child by touching its forehead. *Seven* is the number the habit uses for the register-certified-true-things-of-the-district: the keeper's lamps, the stones' thumb-marks, the gas-lighters, the ledger-*theek-hais*, the *dhumpa* of the termitary — and now, most gravely, Rudra.

The habit gave him its number. The habit *stamped* its habit onto his lower lip, in the count after the count, in the dark, with no voice but its own habit-voice; and Rudra's lip — the register states what is true, which is that the register has no precedent here — *spoke*, unlipped, unvoiced, unwritten, in the grammar-without-grammar that precedes all grammar, the grammar the register calls *the arithmetic-arithmetic*:

**"Saat — kabila; Paanch — warga; Deer — zubaan; Chaar — soch."** *(Seven — the kindred army-house; five — the group of keepers; three — the tongue; four — the thought.)*

And the upward arithmetic of the district's arithmetic-keeper, in his windowless room above — the arithmetic the district had kept running in its flattery-registers, which flattered nobody — recorded, the register now knows, one upward-count at the very same moment Rudra was being made *seven* by the habit: the keeper's own arithmetic, which counted the district's *ordinary* morning entries (cooker-fires, thali-stalls, seed-bags, bullock-bales, the train-whistle which usually arrived at six-fifteen but today arrived at six-seventeen because the habit had, in Rishikesh-the-register's view, though the habit came only from the district's own arithmetic, *held the seven-count's seconds* for its own mirrors to catch up with the arithmetic) — the keeper stopped counting *twice in one day*, which he had never done in thirty years, never once blinked out of his arithmetic to take in the arithmetic of others — but he took it in now, took it up into his mouth, closed his mouth, and said, in the district's grammar of keeping, to nobody, which is the keeper's grammar for *body* itself:

**"Liya aur diya. Ab ghar aa gaya."** *(Taken and given. Now it has come home.)*

---

By noon the district was functioning *on* the count — a count which, for the first time, the district's arithmetic did not audit in the early morning and lay down in the afternoon, but which was being run, unexamined, un-ledgered, unchecked, *through* the district *as* the district, the way water runs through a kasba's private hollow-places the day after the sky stops raining. The district's men were out in the fields — sowing nothing, reaping nothing, standing beside nothing — and the district's women were out in the pit-fields — hulling nothing, winnowing nothing, standing beside nothing — and the district's children were at the banyan — climbing nothing, hiding nothing, standing beside nothing — and every man and woman and child knew, without needing to be told, that *they were all counting the same things at the same time* and that the same things were *being counted back*, and that in this counting there were two registers: one *up*, one *down*. Manikarnika ran the up-count, Rudra was the down-count, and the two registers were now being held together by the habit in a way they had never been held together before — *at the same stair*, at *thirteen* — at the number *seven*, which is the number of all registers at their most complete, and which is apparently — the register is not prepared to say this to every reader at all times but will say it to the reader now because the register is running low on grammar, and the register's grammar is the last armour the district has — which is apparently the district's entire arithmetic, and always has been.

By evening the habit had released the pit-weather. The sky counted *rain* from five until dusk, and the district's arithmetic recorded it *for the last time* — rain did not matter now, or rather rain mattered the way everything else mattered, *together*, in the same *it emerges and is accounted for at the same instant* — the register cannot enter rain under rain, because rain now belongs to *counting*, and counting belongs to *seven*, and seven belongs to *Rudra*. In the register's own vocabulary, rain and Rudra now sit on the same page, which is the register's last page, the page the register cannot turn:

**"Saat ke baad kuch nahi aata — sirf ginti ka khatma."** *(After seven nothing comes — only the count's end.)*

But there was an eighth entry. There is always an eighth entry, in the district's simplest rule of arithmetic — the counting after counting, the *athari*'s breath after the final tally, the ledger's breath after the last ledger-line — and in the district's arithmetic the eighth entry is *never* counted — it is *handed up*. *Handed up to Whom* is the district's oldest obscurity; the register will not say. The district says *eighth* and means *the one who does the handing*. And the reader who is reading this with any head at all for arithmetic has already intuited what the register is about to report: the eighth is *approaching ahead* — the eighth is *approaching* — *approaching* is too soft a word, especially since it comes after *seven*, so the register will say it as the district says it, with the haveli's own voice, as Rukmini said it when Sitara laid her unlit middle lamp on the table at last:

**"Ab aath ka ginti-len-den. Hum — sare — ginte hain. Woh — sare ginta hai. Working order. Tab tak nahi khatam jab tak woh khatam nahi karta. Tab tak hum ginte hain."**

*(Now the eighth's transaction-keeping. We — all of us — count. It — counts everything. Working order. It does not end until it ends it. Until then we count.)*

The test matches: the district said it, the habit said it, the seventh said it, the register said it, and now the eighth is coming, and nothing — no combination of stamp-wick, load-baton, star-ring, wail-lamp, backrooms-own-mother-light, watch-keeper's stamp or pit-field-ba Tablet Almira — nothing is readier than the district's ready arithmetic, which is *this*, and which is done, and which is forward, and which is *coming.*

---

# CHAPTER FIFTY — THE EIGHTH GATE

---

> *"Ginti ka kaam ginna nahi hai — ginti ka kaam ginne wale ko dena hai."*
>
> *— THE WATCHER'S TABLE-BOARD, Turanan, folio of the second hand (final precept)*

---

*(Counting's work is not the counting. Counting's work is to hand the counter onward.)*

---

The morning after Rudra's return opened like every morning — the hullers, the standpipe tea, the deora-cocks, the third lamp's day-shift, the pit-boys dousing their knuckles in karha water to harden them, the habit below breathing four-in four-out against the underside of everything. Nothing announced itself; the district's grammar had outgrown announcements. But the *keeper of arithmetic*, in the windowless room behind the coalsmoke-hut, sat down this day without opening his ledger, and this was such a breach of thirty years' practice that the *second* keeper — his assistant (whose whole qualification was *that she never once knocked*) — actually knocked, and found him with both palms open on the desk, and the ledger's green thread lying beside them, unknotted.

"Ghar aa gaya," said the keeper, without looking up. *(It came home.)*

"Kaam khatam?" she asked. *(Work finished?)*

"Kaam shuru," he said. *(Work beginning.)* And he took up the green thread and — to the open page — tied a *closing* knot, the kind the district's tally-men tie in grain-knots when the season's tally is settled *but not emptied*, and pushed the ledger across the desk toward her, and said the sentence every predecessor-in-office says once in a lifetime and no assistant is ever ready for:

**"Yeh tumhara hai. Mera hisaab unnees baras tha. Ab tumhara shuru. — Aur suno: pehli entry mat likhna. Pehli entry khud likhi hai."**

*(This is yours. My account was nineteen years. Now yours begins. — And listen: do not write the first entry. The first entry is already written.)*

When she opened the ledger to the first fresh page, she found he was right. It was written there, in handwriting she did not recognize — handwriting she would not *learn* to recognize for another seven years, when the same hand turned up on a patrol-report from a district she had not yet been taught to reach — and the handwriting said:

**"Aath. — Chal raha hai."** *(Eight. — It is running.)*

Below the sentence, where the tally-column should have been, was not a number but a name. She does not tell us the name. The register does not ask names from anyone whose ledger has just changed hands; the register's ninth precept says the keeper's first withheld entry is the keeper's own. But the register holds it in reserve, and the district — though no door was knocked on and no drum was beaten — knew by evening that the seventh gate had an eighth opposite it, and that the counting arithmetic was now carrying two ledgers where for nineteen years it had carried one.

---

It was Ram who saw the second ledger's *effects* first, because Ram was the district's old hand at what registers don't say. The register has been careful all along to give Ram very little; the register now pays something back. Ram Vashisht had that morning gone to the west-gate whitewash tally, as he went every morning of the siege eleven days running, and found the beam *unchanged* — where the gate-keeper had entered "Aaj aaya: kuch nahi" *(come today: nothing)* — Ram stood and read it both times, and understood, with the slowness of a man whose whole life had been an exercise in arriving early, that the word *aaya* now had two meanings, and the district was going to spend the next nineteen years finding out whether the second one was *good*.

Then he did the thing his register-file has always known him to do: he didn't write anything. He walked home through the chauk, past the ghee-stained window, up the haveli's three steps, past the second verandah where Sitara was checking lamp-ash, and found Rukmini standing in front of the kitchen fire, holding flatbread like an answer.

"Usne ginti de di?" he asked.

"Tumne ginti *le li*?" she asked back, which was — from her — how *good morning* sounded on the morning the account changed hands.

"Nahi," Ram said. "Ginti uske paas chali gayi. Toh ab ginti *humare paas* kuch nahi rakhti." *(No. The count went off with him. So the count keeps nothing, with us, any more.)*

She set the bread down exactly in the middle of the thaali, and said — for the register's and the district's and the reader's benefit, since all three are owed a clear sentence at this hour — the sentence the register now *hands on*, because Rukmini's sentences have, all through this book, been the only sentences that never need to be transcribed again:

**"Toh ginna band. Milna shuru."** *(Then stop counting. Start meeting.)*

---

That meeting — the first meeting *not as counting* — happened that evening at the haveli's west window, with no lamp between the people who came. Rudra sat on the floor, where during all these years nobody had let him sit, because household duty is the district's finest arithmetic and he had been under it since before he could pronounce his own full name. Sitara trimmed the wick. Rukmini held the flour-sieve, motionless. Ira stood by the door. Veer stood by the other door — Veer always held two walls at once; it was his manner of keeping-books. Aadhya, who came in last, sat directly across from Rudra, close enough to see that the flutter near his lip — the seven-stamp the habit had pressed there, in the arithmetic-arithmetic of the below — had *not* gone away with the light of day.

"Woh nahi gaya," Rudra said, when he finally spoke, and the register enters his sentence *unmetered*, which is how the register enters a heartbeat: **"Woh mujhme baitha hai. Kabhi-kabhi ginti ke bahane — mujhe uthata hai."**

*(It did not leave. It sits inside me. Sometimes, on the excuse of counting — it picks me up.)*

Nobody answered for one full breath. The register, whose business it is to supply grammar at the moment grammar is wanted, supplies only this: the *house* answered, through its seventeen roof-beams, which creaked all together, once, *upward* — the roof-beams' own four-count, upward — and then were still. And the register notes — because it must, because it is the register — that all five people inside the haveli heard the roof-beams *count*, and none of them asked what was being numbered. The district's thirty-one years had taught them when a number belongs to them and when it is only passing *through*.

Finally Ira, from her ledger, whose pages were still clean, read the eighth line — the line the ledger had kept unwritten for her since the night the lamp at the east window of the haveli stopped being necessary:

**"Woh pehle se tha. Tumne use baitne diya."** *(It was there before. You let it sit.)*

The smallest gasp — Sitara's, unnoticed by herself — was the total grant the district's keeper-of-practice would give. The line trembled her hand one notch, and she made one more charcoal entry on the table's underside — *one*, this time, not five or eleven or *seven* — so that the table top had, one over everything, a single notch — and when Rudra stood up, *he stood up on seven*, and when the register looked, afterward, at where he had been standing, the notch in the table's underside had *become eight*.

---

---

# CHAPTER FIFTY-ONE — THE FORM THAT WOULD NOT HOLD

---

> **TRANSMISSION 77 / MIDNIGHT RELAY / DESK OF THE HIGHER COUNTS**
> *"SATWA-CAMP SIGNALS LOST AT GATE SIX. INSTRUMENT READING AT -4 HAS STABILIZED, WHICH IS THE INSTRUMENT'S WAY OF CONFIRMING THE NUMERAL. RECOMMEND: NO ACTION AT GHAT. RECOMMEND: INSTRUMENTS TO THE DISTRICT PROPER. THE DISTRICT WILL DO WHAT DISTRICTS DO WHEN A NUMBER WANTS ANSWERING — THEY WILL TRY TO ANSWER IT IN PUBLIC."*
>
> *— cipher-card received at the Abacus Bureau, folio "CONTINGENT," never answered*

---

The kasba woke before its cocks. At four-fifteen the mill's pressure-gauge knocked against its stop, a buffalo shouldered her stall, and Rudra Vashisht sat upright with the taste of copper in his mouth.

His right hand was already keeping time against his knee.

Four taps. A pause. Four more.

He pressed the hand under his thigh until it stopped. Beyond the window a woman was drawing water; the rope squeaked, a bucket struck brick, and the familiar noises gave him something to listen to that had not come back from the pit with him.

Four days after the storm, the district had begun trying to put its hours in order. The hour before dawn belonged to the teachers. **Guru-ghari**, the kasba called it. Rudra had once liked that hour because nobody expected him to make conversation in it.

Now he waited through nine breaths before getting up.

There was a time-table on the chauk's notice-pin. Vedant had posted it the previous evening, after measuring the board to make certain it would fit between the milk prices and a missing-goat notice.

**SUBAH — PANI — AASMAAN — ZAMEEN**
**DUPAHAR — KHATIYA — PATH — CHAR**
**SHAM — JAL — GAAV — GHAR**

Morning: water, sky, ground. Noon included the charpai, because Vedant considered rest a part of training rather than a reward for surviving it. Evening ended with the village and home. Nobody had been allowed to cross those last two words out.

The curriculum looked simple until one tried to follow it. Carry water without borrowing its weight from the shoulder. Find the same patch of sky after falling. Feel where the ground stopped supporting a movement and began receiving it. Count what the body actually did, not what it had been ordered to do.

Veer arrived with his bag rolled under one arm. He had run through the lanes to warm his old splint-arm, and sweat had darkened his collar despite the hour. Ira carried folded maps under a brass paperweight. Aadhya came with green stains on her thumbs; she had checked the west-threshold plants before coming, although her father's tea would be waiting when she got back.

At the haveli's east window, Rukmini stood with a bundle of wick-reeds. Rudra raised a hand as he passed. She watched until he turned the corner.

The old akhada's praying pit had become their paath-shala. Its deep walls kept the first light out. Four days earlier, the four of them had whitewashed the eastern wall and left their names in the wet plaster. Veer's thumbprint had run through his surname. Ira had made him write Dikshit again, legibly, beneath it.

Those names were the only marks on the wall that did not record a fall.

Vedant waited beside a basin. Sitara had set a tulsi pot on the broad sill near the door. Dhruv came in last, returning from the outer path with his angle-ring tucked under his shirt. His night lesson was over. This morning he had come to watch, not to teach a second pupil beneath the stars.

Four students, three teachers. Ram remained outside, where the door's shade ended.

The sand had been raked level. No weapons lay in it.

Dhruv looked at the four names on the plaster.

"Aaj se ginti poore naam ki hogi. Gin do."

Count the whole name today. Give it.

Veer glanced at Rudra. "Attendance mein bhi practical?"

"Shuru karo," Vedant said.

Veer walked to the centre, knelt, and placed both palms flat on the sand. He did not look at his left forearm, though Rudra saw the small adjustment that kept too much weight off it.

**"Char in. Char out. Char. Ek. Do. Teen. Char. Char."**

The sand underneath him moved.

Not much. Rudra would have missed the first settling if a loose strand from Veer's wrist-wrap had not sunk with it. On the next char-char, a shallow ring formed around each palm. The grains inside the rings lay flat; outside, the rake marks remained.

Veer's breathing steadied. The work travelled up him in visible stages: the wrist ceased trembling, then the elbow unlocked, then his shoulder stopped rising towards his ear.

On the ninth repetition the settling reached his elbow notches. On the eleventh it reached his shoulder. The old injury made him wince, but his hands stayed level.

On the thirteenth the sand stopped.

It did not scatter or harden. It simply refused the next adjustment. Veer drew breath, tried once more, and found his weight resting on two uselessly rigid wrists.

"Bas," Dhruv said.

Veer lifted his hands. Their impressions stayed flat, holding the shape of his palms after the weight had gone.

For a moment nobody moved. Then a thin stream of sand ran into one impression from the edge. Whatever had held it was leaving.

Veer sat back on his heels. His breath came loudly now. He rubbed his left shoulder with his right hand, not pretending that it was merely sweat he wanted to wipe away.

**"Ginti mera roop hai. Chaar mera naam hai. Aage likh do."**

The count is my form. Four is my name. Write onward.

Ira put the paperweight down. "Thirteen pe ruk gaya. Woh bhi likhun?"

"Chhote font mein."

Her mouth moved. Almost a smile.

Vedant crouched beside the two impressions. He ran a finger across their edges; the remaining shape crumbled at once.

"Tumhare jaane ke baad tik nahi paaya," he said.

"Main bhi abhi zyada der tik nahi paaunga," Veer answered.

"Isliye khatiya time-table mein hai."

Rudra laughed with the others. It was a small, ordinary laugh, and he was grateful for it.

Sitara turned the tulsi pot so the morning would eventually reach its shaded side. A root showed through the drainage hole, pale and thread-thin. She laid a piece of broken tile beneath it to catch the water.

"Swaroop," she said, looking from the palm-marks to Veer, "sirf zor ka doosra naam nahi hai. Agar hota, tum abhi tak kar chuke hote."

Veer accepted that without an argument. This worried Rudra more than any boast would have.

From the doorway came the scrape of Ram's sandal. Rudra looked up and found his father watching his hand.

Under the fold of his sleeve, his thumb had begun tapping again.

Four. A pause. Four.

He opened his fingers before anyone could ask him to.

---

Aadhya took the place Veer had left.

At first she wished he had not made it look so nearly possible. The two palm-marks gave her something exact to fail beside. She lowered her hands, spread her fingers, and waited for the familiar warmth to gather beneath her nails.

It came at once.

That was the humiliating part. There was never a shortage at the beginning. She could feel a plant finding water beyond the wall, the damp rag cooling beside Vedant's basin, the tiny living insistence in the tulsi root on the sill. The world did not feel absent. It felt close enough to touch from too many directions.

Green gathered at the base of her right index finger.

She tried to draw it outward, as Sitara had shown her: not a lash, not a vine thrown across a gap, but a continuous line from the ground through the palm. It reached the first joint. The second. A faint shimmer outlined her nail.

Then it died at the fingertip.

Her nail-bed stung. A bead of blood appeared, astonishingly red after the green.

"Phir," she said.

Sitara slid a clean cloth under her hand instead.

"Ek baar aur."

"Pehle dekho hua kya."

"Ruk gaya."

"Kahan?"

Aadhya almost said *in front of everyone*. She pressed the cloth against her finger.

Sitara waited.

"Yahan," Aadhya said, touching the nail.

"Aur tumhara dhyaan?"

Aadhya looked at the wall beyond her hand. She had been imagining the green reaching it, taking the plaster, extending along the cracks. She had not once looked at the skin it would have to pass through first.

Sitara asked her to turn her hand over. The heel of the palm was white from pressure; the wrist had been bent so hard that the tendons stood out.

"Tum darwaza kholte waqt dehleez ko dhakka deti ho?"

"Nahi."

"Toh har baar zameen se ladti kyun ho?"

It was advice Aadhya could understand and could not yet use. On the next attempt she loosened her wrist, thought of the weight passing through it rather than stopping there, and produced a green thread no longer than before. This time it lasted until her little finger cramped.

Nothing transformed. No hidden shape settled over her body. Sweat ran down her ribs as though she had carried water up the ghat.

From the door Veer said, "Mere thirteen tak ka footage delete kar dena."

"Tumhare paas footage hai? Mere paas fingertip hai."

"Close-up. Premium."

She laughed despite herself and ruined the little green thread completely.

Sitara let her sit beside the basin. When Aadhya reached for the water, her injured finger touched its brass rim before her palm did. Pain made her flinch, and a drop landed on the sand.

For one brief instant she felt its coolness under both knees.

She looked down. The drop had soaked away.

"Kya?" Sitara asked.

Aadhya shook her head. Not a discovery yet. She had already mistaken enough beginnings for answers that morning.

At the sill, the tulsi root lay against its scrap of tile. She moved the tile a little so the root would not be crushed when someone opened the door.

---

Ira had drawn a square on the reverse of a spoiled map. She put Veer's brass paperweight in its centre and asked him to move it when she told him to.

"Kitna?"

"Ungli bhar. Kisi bhi taraf."

"Agar mujhe tumse pehle pata nahi toh tumhe kaise—"

"Isi liye."

He held his hand above it. Ira let her gaze soften.

In the last few days she had learned to distinguish the lines that belonged to a thing from the pressure around those lines. A door could be closed and already gathering the movement that would open it. A man could stand still while his weight announced a turn. She had mistaken that announcement for certainty once, during the siege, and somebody had paid for the difference.

This morning she wanted to see the difference before it cost anyone anything.

The paperweight divided.

There were two brass squares now, one resting on the map and one shifted a finger's breadth to the left. Veer's hand had two edges. The lower edge belonged to the man kneeling opposite her; the other crossed the table before he moved.

"Ab," she said.

He moved the weight right.

Her double-sight snapped back so hard that she bit her tongue.

The square on the map became a hole. For a blink she saw the sand through the wooden stool beneath it. Then the stool was solid again, and the room tilted.

Veer caught the basin before her elbow could overturn it.

"Ira?"

"Haath mat hilaana."

He went still.

She counted the real objects aloud: stool, paper, weight, wrist. It was embarrassing. It worked. The white wall returned to its proper distance, and she became aware of blood where her teeth had caught the side of her tongue.

Dhruv knelt outside the square she had drawn.

"Do raaste dikhe?"

She nodded.

"Dono chal chuke the?"

"Nahi."

"Toh dono sach mat likho."

Ira reached for her pencil. The point skittered across the page. Her hand was trembling, not violently enough for anyone to call it an injury and much too violently for her to draw a straight line.

She hated that uncertain middle ground. A broken instrument could be set aside. This one was still attached to her.

She wrote: *One movement seen. Another expected. Expected mistaken for seen.*

Below that she tried to copy the square. The fourth side would not meet the first.

"Aaj ke liye bas," Sitara said.

"Abhi samajh aa raha hai."

"Isliye. Kal bhi samajhna hai."

Veer took his paperweight off the spoiled map. Ira saw his hand once. Only once. She was relieved enough to be angry about it.

Across the room Rudra stood before the basin. His reflection lay on the water without dividing, but the water itself was trembling against the brass.

Nobody had touched it.

Ram stepped in from the doorway.

"Abhi nahi," he said.

Rudra looked over his shoulder. "Maine shuru bhi nahi kiya."

The trembling deepened.

---

Rudra could feel both counts before he tried to use either.

The nine was familiar: the kavach's narrow allowance, the measured space in which he had learned to breathe without driving heat into the seam. He had written its failures in the Load Khata until he knew the shape of the numeral better than his own signature.

The four was not new in the same way. His body recognized it with the awful ease of a path taken in sleep. Since the pit, it had begun arriving before his decisions. A thumb tapping. A heel lifting. Breath returning too soon.

He rested his hands on the basin's rim.

"Sirf paani," he said.

Vedant looked at Ram, then back at Rudra. "Paani ko bhi pata rehna chahiye ki tum chhod sakte ho. Uthao mat. Seedha rakho."

Only keep it level. An exercise for someone on his first week.

Rudra nodded.

The basin stood on a three-legged stool. He did not need to lift anything, stop anything, overcome anything. He had only to keep the little surface of water flat while Vedant slowly drew one leg of the stool over a folded cloth.

One breath.

The stool tilted. The water stayed level.

Two. Heat collected along his forearm, fine and manageable. Gold showed beneath the skin where the seam had already branched towards his shoulder.

Three.

The water developed an edge too straight for a curved basin. Rudra saw the line and knew that he should stop.

At four, his right hand closed by itself.

A red-black film spread across the water. It did not mix with the gold reflected from his arm. The two colours lay beside each other, each making a different claim about where the surface should be.

The gold held it flat. The darker force wanted it standing upright.

"Chhodo," Vedant said.

Rudra opened his hand.

The fingers opened. The force did not leave with them.

He took the fifth breath and felt his chest resist, as though another set of ribs had been fitted inside his own. The nine wanted the slow return he had practised. The four had already begun again, pushing the next intake against the last one's unfinished release.

For an instant he tried the obvious mistake. He tried to make them agree.

He shortened one breath, lengthened another, moved his shoulder as he would adjust a loaded sack. Four and nine were numbers; numbers could be brought onto the same page. There had to be some place where the marks lined up.

The basin screamed against its stool.

Not a loud sound. A thin scrape, metal dragging over metal where no second piece of metal existed.

Aadhya rose from beside the wall.

"Peechhe," Ram said.

She stopped. Rudra hated how quickly she obeyed.

He tried to tell them that he was still there. His tongue would not find the words. The seven-mark at his lower lip had gone numb, and the numbness made his mouth feel like something badly repaired.

The room folded inward.

He was inside the held bell again, without distance or wind, listening to pressure travel through a wall he could not see. In that silence the remembered workshop voice found him.

*Do alag naap ek lakdi pe likhne se jod nahi ban jaata.*

Two measurements on one plank did not make a joint.

He did not answer it. Answering took breath.

Outside, water climbed the near side of the basin. It stood as a trembling plate between his hands. Through it he saw Ira reach for the map and miss its edge; saw Veer moving towards Aadhya instead of towards him; saw the whitewashed names magnified, bent into long unreadable strokes.

Then his own mouth laughed.

A hollow little sound. No breath behind it. Nothing in him had found anything funny.

Ram's hand closed on his shoulder.

Not over the seam. Not on his wrist to force it down. The heel of his father's palm rested on muscle, ordinary weight through the damp cotton of his shirt.

"Pair kahan hai?"

Rudra could not say.

Ram pressed once. "Rudra. Pair."

His left heel was in sand. His right foot stood half on a rake-mark, the little ridge cutting into his sole. He found that ridge with all the attention he had left.

"Yahan," he managed.

"Toh yahin reh. Haath khaali kar."

He had thought his hands were empty already. Now he understood the instruction differently. He stopped trying to hold the water flat. Stopped trying to hold it upright. Stopped, even, trying to make the release look controlled.

The plate fell.

Water struck the rim and burst across the floor. The basin spun out from between his palms. Ram turned his shoulder into it; brass clipped the doorpost, sheared off a strip of fresh plaster, and landed beside the sill.

A crack ran across the whitewashed wall.

Veer had Aadhya behind him. Ira crouched with both hands over her eyes. Sitara's bangles rang once as she caught the tulsi pot before the falling basin could reach it.

Rudra sagged against Ram.

The seam flared from forearm to breastbone. A fresh hairline reached towards his collarbone and stayed there after the light went out.

He smelled scorched cotton. There was a wet patch on Ram's sleeve where the rim had cut him through the cloth.

"Aapko laga?"

"Dekho mujhe."

"Khoon—"

"Dekho."

Rudra looked.

His father was angry. That was almost comforting. Anger belonged to a face he knew; the fear beneath it was harder to bear.

Ram did not count for him. He waited while Rudra took one untidy breath, then another, and did not tell him what number they made.

Outside, the milkman's bell went down the lane. Somebody asked whether the school would open. Somebody else said yes, after the mason had checked the western lintel.

The ordinary morning had continued within earshot.

Rudra could not understand how.

Vedant turned the stool upright. One of its legs had split along the grain. He set it away from the wall so no one would mistake it for sound furniture.

"Do alag hisaab," he said quietly.

Rudra stared at the new line under his collar.

"Mujhe pata hai."

"Abhi pata chala. Pehle tum unko ek hisaab samajh ke theek kar rahe the."

Rudra wanted to argue. It would have meant being able to say what he had been doing, and he could not.

Ram released his shoulder only when he could stand without the hand.

The place where it had rested felt colder than the wet shirt around it.

---

By noon, Rudra had written three lines in the Load Khata and crossed out two.

*I lost the count* was wrong. He had known exactly where both counts were.

*I could not hold it* was worse. He had held it after being told to let go.

He left the third line alone.

*My hand opened. The work did not stop.*

Ram sat opposite him at the haveli's kitchen table while Rukmini washed the cut in his upper arm. It was shallow. Rudra had checked that three times before she told him to stop inspecting her patient.

"Kapda kharaab ho gaya," Ram said.

"Kapda toh pehle bhi kharaab tha."

"Achha wala pehenta toh—"

She pulled the bandage tight enough to finish that thought for him.

They were talking about a shirt. Rudra could hear all the things they had agreed not to say in front of him, and all the older things they had never agreed on at all.

Rukmini tied the knot. Her thumb remained on Ram's pulse for a moment after there was any reason to keep it there. Then she collected the stained water and went out.

"Aaj band," Ram said.

"Maine phir maanga?"

"Nahi."

"Toh phir?"

Ram looked at the open khata. "Main apne liye bol raha hoon."

Rudra lowered his eyes first.

In the afternoon the district obeyed the middle line of Vedant's time-table. Veer slept on a charpai with a rolled towel beneath his shoulder. Ira sat in the shade, dictating corrections to Chandan because her hand still shook. Aadhya helped Rukmini separate clean strips of cloth from those that had only one more washing left in them.

Nobody called it rest. Everyone did it.

Rudra lay awake and listened to someone repairing the paath-shala doorpost. Hammer, pause, hammer. Each pause was different. He tried not to be relieved by that.

---

At sunset Vedant allowed them onto the haveli's flat roof. He had inspected it first, to Veer's enormous and briefly concealed disappointment.

"Soch rahe the seedhi bhi naapoge," Veer said.

"Naapi thi. Tum so rahe the."

All four sat with their backs against the low parapet. Ram stood by the stairhead. Below them the district's daylight lamps were becoming ordinary evening lamps without being extinguished in between.

From this height Rudra could see part of the west road. Aadhya's tulsi half-ring looked thin after the siege, with bare gaps where pots had broken. The milestone threw a long shadow into it.

"Wahan khaali jagah hai," he said.

Aadhya followed his gaze. "Kal laga dungi."

"Maine paudhe ke liye nahi—"

"Pata hai. Main paudhe ke liye bol rahi hoon."

He leaned his head back against the plaster. She was not offering him a lesson. She had plants to replace. It was a relief not to be made into a meaning for once.

Ira unfolded the spoiled map. "Subah jo hua, uske liye hum ek signal rakhenge. Bolna mushkil ho toh bhi chale."

"Main haath khol raha tha."

"Dikha nahi," Veer said. "Paani ke peechhe tha."

That stopped him.

They settled on the heel of his foot scraping twice. Not a breath, not a hand gesture: something the force did not already seem to be borrowing. Ram made him test it on the roof. The noise reached the courtyard clearly.

Rukmini looked up from below. "Kisne khurcha? Abhi lep hua tha."

All five looked away with an obedience the morning's instructions had failed to produce.

A sharp click came from the eastern parapet.

Mortar chipped away beside a newly fitted rain bracket. The piece fell towards the courtyard. Ram caught the edge of Rudra's shirt before he could lunge after it; below, Rukmini stepped back as the chip broke on the stone where her foot had been.

For a moment nobody spoke.

Then she looked up again, this time without annoyance.

Vedant was at the bracket. Under its lip, almost hidden by wet mortar, lay a copper washer crossed by a narrow slit. A transparent filament ran from it into a hole drilled through the parapet.

"Haath mat lagana," he said.

Rudra had already withdrawn his hand.

Vedant lifted the bracket with the wooden handle of his measuring spoon. The washer turned freely inside it, although the bracket itself was fixed. Its slit faced the stairhead where Ram stood.

He moved the wood across the slit. Somewhere within the wall, a small spring clicked twice.

A surveyor's fitting. Small enough to be delivered with repairs, ordinary enough for a mason not to question.

Ira crouched well away from it. "Humko dekh raha tha?"

"Kya naap raha tha, abhi nahi pata," Vedant said.

He put a clay cup over it rather than pull it out.

Rudra stared at the covered thing. Somebody had found a way to take a measurement inside his home. He could see the little cup move each time the hidden spring clicked beneath it.

Ram went downstairs to Rukmini.

Nobody offered to resume training.

---

In a windowless office, a paper tape advanced one tooth. An attendant fed its reading into a punch. The resulting card went beneath the earlier message, **GINTI NE DHVANI SUDHAAR LIYA**, in a tray labelled DISTRICT.

The new card bore two groups of holes and a printed instruction:

**ALAG RAKHO. PHIR NAAPO.**

Keep separate. Measure again.

No name appeared beside the order.

---

Sitara had brought the tulsi pot back from the paath-shala. It stood on the haveli's lower verandah, on the same broken tile Aadhya had moved that morning.

Rudra found her crouched beside it after supper.

"Abhi bhi duty?" he asked.

"Iska kal se paani galat taraf bhaag raha hai."

She tipped the pot slightly and let the last water drain. Her bandaged index finger stuck out from the others, clumsy as a borrowed tool.

Rudra sat on the step. "Tumhara haath bhi meri wajah se—"

"Subah tumse pehle meri baari thi. Credit mat lo."

He looked at her. She went on studying the soil.

"Mujhe laga tha aaj ho jayega," she said at last.

"Mujhe bhi."

"Tumhara nahi. Mera."

"Haan. Woh bhi."

She smiled, but it passed quickly. He did not try to bring it back.

From inside came the scrape of Rukmini's ladle against a pot. Sitara trimmed the middle lamp, the one still kept for whoever stood between departure and arrival. Its small flame bent towards the verandah and straightened.

Then the tulsi pot cracked.

One dry sound. A line opened from the drainage hole to the rim.

Aadhya caught the two sides before the soil could spill. Green showed in the opening, not the shimmer she had forced towards her fingertip, but something soft and folded, pushing slowly past the clay.

A new shoot.

It had come through the crack rather than climbing towards the mouth of the pot.

Aadhya held her breath. The split widened against her palms.

"Mat jodo," Sitara said from the doorway.

Aadhya looked up.

"Isko jagah chahiye."

Behind her, beneath the threshold stone, something tugged once at the roots outside.

---

---

# CHAPTER FIFTY-TWO — THE ROOT THROUGH THE CRACK

---

> *"The roots were present before the failure. So were the drain, the loose stones, and the people waiting to cross.*
> *The repair account must not describe the girl as additional material. Her hands were injured in the work."*
>
> *— west-threshold inquiry, correction entered after the first report was returned unsigned*

---

By sunrise Aadhya had learned that a cracked pot could cut through cloth without making a sound.

A wet line spread across the sling beneath it. She adjusted the bundle against her hip, felt the broken rim press through to her skin, and stopped trying to carry it like an unbroken thing.

"Neeche se," Sitara said.

Aadhya put both palms beneath the base. The cloth stopped pulling against the crack.

They had kept it in a shallow grain tray overnight, with loose soil banked around the split. The new shoot had opened two small leaves. It grew sideways through the clay, then turned upwards. One leaf was caught against the edge.

Aadhya wanted to free it with her injured index finger. She used the corner of a clean reed instead.

There had been no second shimmer in her hand. No answer to her attempt to summon the green before bed. Whatever had happened to the pot had not left her with a new obedience she could command.

That should have disappointed her. Mostly she was tired of wanting the right thing so fiercely that she stopped seeing what was in front of her.

At the kitchen door Rukmini gave them two rotis folded around salt and onion. Sitara took both portions because Aadhya's hands were full.

"Ghar hote jaana," Rukmini told her. "Tumhare pitaji kal bhi poochh rahe the."

"Pata hai."

"Unko bhi pata hai ki tumko pata hai. Isse kaam nahi chalta."

So they stopped at the Sharma house on the way to the west threshold.

Her father had kept the tea on the unlit side of the stove. He did not ask whether she had succeeded. He looked at the cloth around her finger, then moved the cup to the side where she could reach it with her left hand.

She set the tulsi bundle on the floor.

"Iska gamla toot gaya," she said.

"Dikh raha hai."

"Phenkna nahi tha."

He crouched, studied the little shoot, and brought an old steel plate from the cupboard. He put it beneath the bundle so the broken clay could not cut her again.

"Paudha bachana hai toh gamla sar pe leke mat ghoomo. Jagah do."

It was very like Sitara's advice. From him it sounded less mysterious and much harder to evade.

Aadhya drank the cold tea. Her father talked about a milk bill someone had paid twice and a sack of flour that had arrived damp. Before the siege she would have waited for an opening to leave. This morning she listened until he finished.

When they rose, he tied the plate to the cloth sling himself. The knot was so thorough that Sitara raised an eyebrow.

"Ab kaatna padega," Aadhya said.

"Toh kaat lena."

He did not let go until she had both hands properly underneath.

---

The west-threshold half-ring had lost more pots than it had plants. After the siege, people had pushed the surviving root-balls together in the shade of the milestone, then gone to help where the injuries were human.

Aadhya could not blame them. She had done the same.

Now the exposed roots had begun drying into one another. Tulsi stems leaned across marigold leaves; a length of grass had found the damp under a cracked brick. The half-ring looked untidy and stubbornly alive.

Beyond it the road dipped over a masonry drain. The retaining wall on the riverward side had been patched after the fighting, leaving a narrow passage for handcarts. Two women waited there with water cans while a boy guided a loaded cart across the planks.

Aadhya knelt in the bare place nearest the milestone.

The soil felt cool on top and wrong beneath.

She kept her palm down. A little lower there should have been the slow dampness she knew from watering. Instead she felt a pull, as if the roots were holding on to water that was being taken away from them.

"Kal raat bhi," she said.

Sitara lowered herself beside her without asking her to explain the unfinished sentence.

They set the pot down. Aadhya cut her father's knot, folded the cloth away from the crack, and tried to ease the plant free. The soil held together; the root through the drainage hole would not pass back through it.

"Toh toda jaaye?" Sitara asked.

Aadhya looked at her.

"Gamla," Sitara clarified.

"Pata hai."

Still she hesitated. Something she had carried so carefully ought not to be broken on purpose. She hated finding that little feeling in herself, as if the care had been for the clay all along.

She pressed her palm against one side and broke off a loose piece.

The tulsi came away with most of its soil. The new shoot lay undamaged across her wrist.

Under the root-ball, where the dark earth met the old drainage hole, the roots were curled tightly round themselves.

"Rasta tha," Aadhya said. "Main upar dekh rahi thi. Yeh neeche dhoondh raha tha."

She lowered it into the gap in the half-ring. Instead of packing the soil flat, she worked it between the roots a little at a time, leaving room for water to enter.

Sitara gave her the lota.

Aadhya poured. Water darkened the soil, paused, and vanished too quickly.

Both women watched the wet patch shrink.

Down at the drain mouth a thin stream had begun running out of a joint that should have been dry.

Aadhya put her palm back on the ground. The pull beneath it strengthened.

At the crossing, a cart wheel bumped the patched wall. Dust sifted from between two stones.

She stood so fast that the lota tipped over.

"Cart roko," she said.

The boy guiding it looked at her hand, saw no green, and looked away.

Aadhya stepped into the road and caught the wooden shaft with both hands.

"Roko. Neeche se mitti jaa rahi hai."

This time he felt the tremor through the wood too.

---

They unloaded the cart before moving it.

The boy protested about the sacks getting dirty. One of the women waiting with water cans told him dirt could be brushed off and a leg could not. She carried the first sack herself. By the time the others joined in, Sitara had stretched a rope across the approach.

Aadhya walked the edge on hands and knees.

The drain ran from a shallow settling basin beyond the road, under the patched crossing, and out towards the lower river path. She had watered the plants from that basin for weeks. Now its surface was sinking visibly, leaving a wet brown band on the stone.

A mason came from the houses with his tool bag half closed. He pressed the handle of a hammer against the retaining wall, listened, and stopped the cart boy from climbing down for a better look.

"Paani mitti kheench raha hai. Patthar abhi baithe nahi."

The stones had not moved yet. The word *yet* changed how everyone stood.

Sitara sent the boy for Vedant. Aadhya asked the women to move people back from the road, then knelt beside the first exposed root-ball.

She could make a plant hold water. She had done it before without knowing what to call it. If the roots could keep some of what the drain was taking, the soil might stop slipping long enough for the mason to brace the wall.

She set her uninjured hand among them.

Warmth gathered. Green reached the first leaves and brightened them, absurdly pretty in the dust.

Nothing happened below.

She shifted closer, let her knees settle, tried to feel the place where the little feeder roots met the deeper ones. The leaves lifted higher. Water beaded along their edges.

The wet band in the basin continued falling.

She pulled her hand away before she wasted any more.

"Upar aa raha hai," she said.

Sitara nodded.

"Maine neeche bheja tha."

"Tumne bheja. Unhone liya. Dono ek baat nahi hain."

Aadhya wanted, briefly and viciously, for somebody to give her an instruction that did not have two halves.

Then a woman behind the rope asked if the east lane was still clear, and the feeling passed. Aadhya knew the answer to that. She got up and gave it.

Vedant arrived carrying his basin and a length of clear pipe. Rudra and Veer came behind him. Rudra stopped when he saw the broken pot's cloth on the ground, then followed Aadhya's pointing hand to the drain instead.

"Kya chahiye?" he asked.

Not *shall I try*. She was grateful enough not to look at him for too long.

"Woh planks. Seedhe nahi, peechhe se. Zameen beech mein khokhli hai."

He and Veer took the planks round the safe side. They laid them where the mason indicated, spreading the load over stones that still held.

Vedant lowered one end of the clear pipe into the settling basin. Water rose in it, stopped, then jerked backwards.

His expression changed.

Under the drain's outer grille, a brass tongue had been pushed between two stones. Most of it lay in shadow. Aadhya could see a little slit on the exposed end, like the fitting on the haveli roof.

"Mat kheenchna," Vedant told the mason.

"Yahi toh atka hai."

"Atka nahi. Lagaya hai."

He used a stick to show them the thin cord running from the tongue into the closed service channel. It was tight enough to hum when he touched the stone beside it.

Rudra stepped back of his own accord.

The sight hurt Aadhya more than his seam had. A month ago she would have thought caution the opposite of courage in him. Now he stood holding a plank while something inside his sleeve lit and faded, and she understood the effort of remaining useful without being allowed to be spectacular.

She took the other end of the plank.

"Neeche rakhte hain," she said.

Together they slid it to the mason.

---

For the next half-hour the work was almost ordinary.

They dug a shallow diversion upstream of the settling basin. The water-can women carried away the loose earth. Veer and Rudra held the brace while the mason packed wedges beneath it. Sitara worked beside Aadhya, opening little pockets around the root-balls so that they would not be torn when the soil shifted.

Aadhya's injured fingertip burned each time grit entered the dressing. She changed hands rather than ask for a fresh cloth; when Sitara noticed, she changed the cloth for her without comment.

The new tulsi shoot stood in the middle of the loosened bed. Its two leaves did not face the sun. They faced the drain.

Aadhya touched the soil beside it.

This time she did not push warmth towards the leaves. She followed the cool path the water had made. It passed below the tulsi, under a buried root thicker than her thumb, and towards the old tree on the lower path.

She had walked over that root every day without noticing it.

Something answered from farther away than her hand could reach. The tree's leaves rattled, though the morning air was still.

Aadhya took her palm away in alarm.

"Uska tha," she said. "Maine kheench liya."

Sitara looked towards the tree. "Wapas kar sakti ho?"

Aadhya tried. A little of the warmth left her wrist. The leaves steadied. Her injured nail began bleeding again.

There was the route. There was the price of taking it in one direction.

Before she could say so, Vedant called for everyone to move back.

The diversion had filled. For a moment its shallow current ran exactly where they had dug it. Then the water stopped at the far end as cleanly as if someone had put a lid against it.

The brass tongue under the grille clicked.

Aadhya felt the buried root pull hard against her knee.

The settling basin emptied in one long gulp.

The mason's wedges shot out of the wall.

One struck the rope-post. The rope fell, and the people waiting behind it stepped back into the mouth of the east lane, where a loaded handcart had just arrived from the other side.

For an instant the two streams of people could not pass each other.

The retaining wall leaned towards them.

---

The front of the wall tipped riverward.

Behind it, the last intact paving slab sank at one end and rose at the other. Its lifted edge caught the handcart's axle. The cart swung sideways across the east lane, trapping the waiting people between its load and the house-wall.

Veer reached the axle first. He got his good shoulder beneath the shaft and stopped it crushing a woman's knee. Rudra hauled a sack off the top. The shifting load gave them a gap no wider than a forearm.

Then another stone dropped out of the retaining wall.

The gap closed.

Aadhya felt the movement through her knees before she saw it. The buried root was being stretched across an opening in the soil. If it tore, the tree would go with the wall, and the lower path would take the whole weight.

She put her palm over it.

Green ran through the nearest leaves and stopped at the edge of the broken ground.

Her old reach. Enough to lift a plant. Not enough to keep a road beneath people's feet.

"Aadhya, peechhe," Sitara said.

There was time to get back. Aadhya knew that. Sitara was not asking her to abandon anyone; she was making room for a second attempt that might not kill the person attempting it.

But the route was already under Aadhya's hand.

Not a line she had to throw across empty air. The root crossed the break. The soil rested on it. Farther down, the tree's weight held it in place. Water ran along the underside, taking a little earth each time it passed.

She had been trying to make the plants obey the shape of her reach.

She turned her hand so the whole palm touched.

Her other hand went to the old threshold stone beside the milestone. Sun had warmed its upper face; underneath, the root touched the damp foundation. One palm on living ground, one on the place where people crossed it.

For once she did not send the green ahead.

She let the strain arrive.

It struck through her wrist like a cart rolling over bone.

Her shoulders folded. Her injured nail split to the quick. She heard herself make a thin animal sound and would have pulled away if the next movement had not come from her feet: toes spreading against earth, knees taking weight, back straightening just enough to give the force somewhere else to go.

The green passed her fingertip without stopping there.

It climbed her arms in fine branching lines. Not light resting on skin: a change beneath it, warm channels opening from palm to elbow to shoulder, her whole body answering where one exhausted hand had failed. She could feel the root's far end as distinctly as the stone beneath her left thumb.

The air smelled of bruised tulsi.

Aadhya lifted her head.

**"SHAKTI: ADI SHAKTI."**

Blood ran from beneath three nails at once. Down on the lower path, the old tree shed a scatter of yellowing leaves.

The root tightened.

The road did not leap back into place. It stopped moving for half a heartbeat, lurched, and held again. Aadhya felt each stone drag against the rootwork beneath it. Every shift threatened to tear her palms apart.

"Ek-ek," she said.

Nobody heard.

Sitara turned towards the cart. "Ek-ek niklo. Bhaagna nahi."

Rudra lifted the sacks down one at a time. Veer lowered the axle enough for the first woman to slip past. Behind her, the cart boy pressed himself against the house-wall and pulled a smaller child through the gap by the back of his shirt.

The last woman's water can caught between the axle and the slab.

"Chhod do," Aadhya said.

The woman did not let go. It was a good can, soldered twice, half her household's stored water inside it.

Aadhya could not blame her for the half-second it took to choose.

Then the handle tore. The woman fell backwards into Rudra's arms, and the can burst against the stone.

Water ran over Aadhya's left palm.

She let it pass into the dry soil instead of pulling it towards the plant. The buried root eased by a fraction. More roots answered beneath the adjoining bed: existing threads, fine and pale, taking the strain into a wider patch of ground.

The new tulsi shoot bent flat. Aadhya felt the tiny stem begin to tear and loosened her grip on that part of the bed. It sprang up again, shaking.

She could not ask one new thing to carry everything.

"Neeche wala rasta khaali?" she called.

The mason shouted back. Clear.

"Tree ke paas mat jaana."

He caught the cart boy before he could run that way.

Aadhya drew on the broader root beneath her right hand. The tree's leaves rustled hard. Its bark roughened against a pressure she could feel in her own forearms, and the warmth rising into her was no longer gentle.

Too much taken in one direction.

She pressed her left palm harder against the threshold. Let the heat travel out with the water. Let the wet earth receive what the leaves could not spare.

The brass tongue beneath the grille rang like a spoon struck against a cup.

Vedant looked from it to her hands.

"Roko mat," he said. "Dhalne do."

She did. Not away from the people, not back into herself: down the open diversion they had already dug.

The small current broke through its invisible stop. Muddy water struck the far bank and ran free.

Vedant cut the cord with the mason's chisel.

The brass tongue snapped sideways. A narrow piece of its edge peeled back, red with heat, and the pull beneath Aadhya's knees slackened.

"Ab," she whispered.

Veer shoved the empty cart into the lane. Rudra and the mason dragged the brace clear. The last man stepped onto solid paving.

Aadhya let the road go.

The riverward wall collapsed in three heavy blows. Dust struck the half-ring. A slab rolled down the empty lower path and stopped against the base of the tree.

Her arms were still green.

For one terrified instant she thought she would not be able to stop either.

She loosened her fingers one by one. Lifted the uninjured edge of each palm. Allowed the warmth to leave her shoulders before taking her knees off the ground.

The branching lines faded towards her wrists.

She could no longer feel her right thumb.

When she tried to stand, the leg beneath her folded. Sitara caught her under the arms and lowered her onto the threshold stone.

The people were on the safe side. The water was running down the dug channel. Aadhya looked from one fact to the other, needing both before she could believe she had finished.

Then she bent over her hands and was sick into the dust.

---

She did not hear the cheering until someone asked people to stop it.

Aadhya sat with her back against the milestone while Sitara washed grit from her hands. Her right thumb lay across her lap at an angle that looked wrong, although she could move it when she watched closely. She simply could not feel it move.

The index finger beside it was going numb too.

"Wapas aa jayega?" she asked.

Sitara examined the nail-bed before answering. "Abhi bandhaai karte hain. Uske baad dekhte hain."

Aadhya looked away. She preferred the honest answer to reassurance and wished, bitterly, that honesty felt better.

The woman whose water can had broken came through the crowd carrying its flattened handle. She kept turning it over, trying to understand where it might be soldered again.

"Kisi ko laga?" Aadhya asked her.

"Meri kohni chhili. Aur tum—"

"Bachcha?"

"Ghar bhej diya."

Aadhya nodded. The woman crouched, set the broken handle beside her, and touched her shoulder once before leaving.

It was the only thanks she could take without wanting to cry.

Across the road, Rudra and the mason were checking the lower path from a safe distance. Veer held up the fallen rope for people to pass under on their way to the longer eastern route. Nobody needed green hands to do that work. Nobody could have crossed without it.

Aadhya watched until her stomach settled.

"Kitni der?" she asked.

"Tumhari?" Sitara said. "Log nikal sake. Itni."

"Mujhe measurement chahiye."

"Phir Vedant se poochhna. Mujhe tumhara chehra dikh raha tha."

Aadhya leaned her head back against the milestone. The stone was warm. For the first time that day she felt only stone, not everything touching it underneath.

---

By afternoon, the half-ring was being dug out carefully rather than admired.

The root she had used lay just below the surface. It was thicker near the tree and badly stripped where the paving had shifted against it. The mason wrapped that place in damp sacking, more out of respect than knowledge. Aadhya asked him not to pack the earth too tightly over it.

He listened. That almost undid her again.

The tree had lost a whole patch of leaves on its riverward side. Its branches there looked thin enough for the afternoon sky to show through. The new tulsi shoot stood upright, alive, with a brown line where it had bent.

She had not made a road from nothing. She could see, now, what had carried the road and what the carrying had taken.

Vedant set the recovered brass tongue on the steel plate her father had provided. He had wrapped its cut cord around a stick so nobody would touch the bare end.

"Paani band nahi kiya tha," he said. "Doosri taraf ka dabaav badhaya. Humne bypass khola toh usko bhi pakad liya."

Aadhya followed his finger along the peeled metal. A row of little dents marked its underside. Their spacing changed halfway down.

"Tune kiya tha?" Ira asked.

She had arrived with two volunteers and a stack of diversion notices. Her pencil was tied to a strip of cloth so she would not keep dropping it.

"Dobara set hua," Vedant said. "Kab, abhi nahi kahunga."

He did not call it a trap built for Aadhya. The evidence was not yet that kind. A fitting had altered under pressure, and their attempted repair had been anticipated. That was enough to make the recovered piece dangerous.

Ira made a copy of the marks without opening her second sight. The line wandered once. She left the error visible and labelled it.

Rudra watched from the edge of the group, his sleeve rolled low over the new seam.

"Aaj raat mat try karna," Aadhya told him.

He looked offended. "Main paani peene aaya tha."

She moved the lota towards him, then almost knocked it over with her numb thumb.

He caught it by the rim. Neither of them commented.

After a moment he said, "Jo naam bola tumne—"

She waited.

"Yaad tha?"

Aadhya looked at her palms. She could remember the shape of the work more clearly than the saying of the words: two hands receiving the same strain, the root's far end no longer impossibly far away.

"Us waqt sahi laga," she said. "Abhi bolungi toh sirf shabd honge."

He accepted that. He of all people knew the difference between knowing a count and being able to live inside it.

---

Her father found her at the haveli before evening.

He brought no flowers and asked no one what the district was calling her. He set a cup on the table, saw her try to grip its handle, and put a small bowl beside it instead.

Aadhya stared at the bowl.

"Dono haath se," he said.

She lifted it between the heels of her palms. Warm tea touched her mouth. The effort required such care that she could not speak until she had set it down.

"Plate wahan reh gayi."

"Le aayenge."

"Uspe woh cheez rakhi hai."

"Toh baad mein le aayenge."

He sat while she finished. When the tea cooled, he did not offer to replace it. She drank what he had made.

Outside, Veer was arguing with a volunteer who wanted to repaint the diversion sign with a new name for the crossing. Ira was making him keep the practical directions legible. Sitara had gone to check the tree's exposed root before the light failed.

Aadhya could hear all of them. For once she did not have to go and help.

Her father untied the empty sling. The knot he had made that morning lay cut in the middle of the cloth.

He smoothed it flat with his thumb.

---

At dusk the crossing received its first official notice. ROAD CLOSED. EAST LANE OPEN. NO CARTS AFTER DARK.

Below it, in smaller writing, Aadhya Sharma had signed for the placement of the rope. Her letters were awkward because she had used her left hand.

The rumours travelled faster than the notice. At the milk stall they said a tree had lifted the road. At the well they said a girl had lain underneath it. The mason, asked for the fourth time, said that he had seen two hands on the ground and people walking past. He would swear to that much.

Farther downriver, at the Machli bend, the iron landing-gate had been checked and tied off before the lamps were lowered for Dhruv's night lesson.

Its wheel was locked. Its handle was empty.

Nevertheless, as the first star showed above the oxbow, the gate's counterweight rose one notch.

Then another.

---

---

# CHAPTER FIFTY-THREE — WHAT RISES FROM THE FALL

---

> *"The witness recorded how many times he got up. The account omits where he put his feet.*
> *At the end, he no longer returned to the place from which he had fallen. He stood where the others needed a way through."*
>
> *— Turanan landing inquiry, appended to the manual of returning rather than the book of victories*

---

That afternoon, before the gate at the Machli bend moved, Veer had been unable to button his own cuff.

The button was small. His fingers were large. His left forearm, which had spent the morning helping to hold a cart above a woman's knee, had begun its old deep ache. None of these facts ought to have felt like an accusation.

He left the cuff open.

On his bed the steel tin lay beside a clean wrist-wrap. He lifted out his grandfather's manual, meaning to read one page before the night lesson. The book opened where it always did.

*Pehle: tan. Doosra: disha. Teesra: dono jhooth.*

His grandmother's green correction remained underneath. He did not need to run a finger along it any more. He could have traced the words with his eyes closed.

He understood them. He had stood empty-handed at the ghat, after the baton broke, and understood them with his whole frightened body.

So why had thirteen repetitions in a sand pit reduced him to a pair of rigid wrists?

He shut the manual before the question could turn into an answer borrowed from somebody else's handwriting.

Aadhya had not looked victorious when they brought her back. She had looked grey around the mouth and furious at a cup. Veer kept seeing the right thumb that did not know where the handle was.

He was glad she had done it. That gladness was uncomplicated.

The fear beside it was not. The next time she had to hold a road, he wanted to be able to give her somewhere to put it down.

He put the manual back in the tin, wrapped his arm without overtightening it, and went out.

---

Ira was at the lower verandah, sorting bandages for the night stations. The tremor had made folding a slow business. She had solved it by holding one end beneath her paperweight and drawing the other towards her.

"Turanan?" she asked.

"Haan."

"Seedha jaoge?"

"Thoda London ghoom ke."

She looked at him until he regretted the joke.

"Landing pe rakh dena," she said, pushing a cloth parcel towards him. "Upar wala relief bench. Main notices leke aati hoon."

He tested the parcel's weight. "Isme aadmi toh nahi?"

"Tumhare liye bandage zyada rakhe hain."

He wanted to say she would not need them. Instead he asked her to fasten the pin on his wrist-wrap. She did it carefully, avoiding the place where the skin had thickened under the old splint.

"Dheela toh nahi?"

"Theek hai."

Her hand remained near the pin.

"Khulta dikhe toh dobara baandh lena," she said.

"Lesson mein?"

"Jahan khule."

He could hear the other instruction in it. Do not pretend the fastening is sound because people are watching.

He tucked the parcel beneath his good arm. "Tum bhi pencil girne do kabhi. Har baar pakadna zaroori nahi."

She moved the paperweight off the cloth. Her fingers shook. The fold stayed.

"Jao," she said.

---

The river bent east beyond Machli-ghat, turned round the old rock, and returned upon itself in the oxbow. In the last light, the current looked as though it had reconsidered a decision.

Veer left the bandages at the upper relief bench. Ira would work there with the night-watch clerk, well away from the training pit. Dhruv's lesson still had one student, no torch, and no audience to impress.

Bhanu met him at the akhada's river door.

"Aaj bhi barabar?" the wrestler asked.

"Aaj khud se haarne aaya hoon. Suna hai expert yahin milte hain."

Bhanu grinned and slapped the uninjured shoulder.

He had been helping repair the landing below the akhada. Through the open wall Veer could see its iron gate, raised between two stone guides over the narrow gangway. A counterweight hung in a shaft on the bank side, joined to the gate by chains passing over an overhead wheel. The gate kept loaded cargo from rolling down onto a boat before the boatmen were ready.

Tonight the cargo had been cleared. A few families still waited on the floating landing beyond it for the late ferry. The watchman had tied the gate up as an extra precaution and locked the wheel.

Bhanu showed Veer the knot because he was proud of it.

"Ira se seekha?" Veer asked.

"Meri maa se."

"Same syllabus."

Dhruv called his name from the pit.

The light had thinned enough to leave only the sky's reflection on the river. Veer put his bag at the wall, stepped onto the sand, and found the first star without trying to name it.

"Giro," Dhruv said.

Veer lowered himself.

"Woh baithna hua."

So Bhanu threw him.

The fall drove air out of his chest. He lay still for the two breaths the akhada allowed, set his heel, and came up in the familiar wave from ankle to shoulder.

Bhanu caught the shoulder before it completed the turn and put him down again.

"Pehli jagah pe kyun laut rahe ho?" Dhruv asked.

Veer spat a grain of sand off his lip. "Wahin se gira tha."

"Woh jagah ab uske paas hai."

Bhanu stood exactly where Veer had been standing. His feet had not moved since the throw.

Veer looked from the feet to the strip of empty sand behind them.

He had learned how to rise. Without noticing, he had made a habit of rising into the same answer.

Down at the landing, a chain clicked.

Bhanu turned towards the sound at once.

---

Bhanu was halfway down the steps when the chain clicked again.

The counterweight had risen two notches inside its shaft. The gate had come down by the same amount. Its securing rope was stretched tight enough to flatten against the iron bar.

Bhanu checked the locked wheel, then put a hand on the rope and took it away sharply.

"Sabko upar lao," he told the watchman.

Nobody argued about a ferry fare. The watchman opened the side wicket and began bringing the waiting families towards the gangway. Bhanu wedged a timber beneath the gate while Veer got to his feet.

Dhruv was beside him before he reached the steps.

"Lesson khatam," the teacher said.

Below, the oxbow had changed colour. A narrow dark run crossed the reflected sky, moving towards the landing against the slower surface current. At its far end a low service launch lay almost motionless beside a mooring post. A man on it leaned over a metal case.

Veer could see his hands. One held the case open. The other turned a short handle inside it.

No shout came from the launch. No explanation.

The landing-gate dropped hard onto Bhanu's timber.

Wood cracked. The securing rope parted with a noise like a palm striking water.

Veer reached the lower bar before it could take the gangway. His left arm caught too much weight. He shifted it onto his good shoulder, bent his knees, and drove upwards.

For a moment the gate rose.

"Nikaalo," he said.

Bhanu pulled the first two people through. An old man came after them on a stick, moving as fast as his knees allowed. The next family stayed back to give him room.

It would have worked if the gangway had been on solid ground.

Its outer end rested on the floating landing. The landing's upstream mooring line had begun to slip through a cleat that should have been pinned. Each time Veer pushed against the gate, his feet pushed the gangway farther down.

The gap above him narrowed.

Dhruv caught the mooring line before the pin tore free. He wrapped it round a stone bollard, turned his angle-ring flat against the top, and drew the loose span towards him.

Pale light touched the line. Its wet fibres stopped sliding.

The floating landing still yawed in the current. Dhruv could hold its distance from the bank; he could not make its deck stay level as well.

"Bank main rok raha hoon," he called. "Pair ki jagah badlo."

Veer tried to set his right foot nearer the bank.

The gangway rolled beneath it.

He fell to one knee. The gate struck his shoulder, and the brief pale shell of VELUM flared over his back, breaking the impact into a dozen smaller pains. Metal scraped cloth. Something warm ran down his shin.

He pushed up from the knee.

The gate rose with him, just enough for Bhanu to bring the old man's stick through. Then it came down again, heavier than before.

On the launch, the short handle turned.

Veer saw it between the bars. Anger helped him straighten his back and did nothing useful for the left forearm.

He could have dropped out from beneath the gate and gone after the man. The bank path was clear. Three strides, a jump from the mooring post, and the metal case would be within reach.

Behind him a child asked whether the boat was still coming.

Veer put his shoulder back under the bar.

"Bhanu. Kitne?"

"Chhe. Aur main."

The watchman had stayed on the floating side to help the last family. The six included him. Veer checked that without turning his head: two small pairs of feet, three adults, one bare-legged watchman.

He did not look at the launch again.

---

He rose the way he had practised.

Heel, ankle, knee, hip, shoulder. The wave came cleanly through his body. The gate lifted a hand's breadth.

Then the chain tightened and sent the same weight straight down through him into the floating gangway.

His knees bent. The iron returned to his shoulder. The people behind him had gained no room at all.

He tried a second time, letting the old forearm stay close instead of extending it. Better for the arm. No better for the people.

Sand had accepted the force beneath his palms that morning. The gangway could not. Every perfect rise was pushing away the floor he needed.

Bhanu threw a rope round the gate's side bar and hauled from the bank. It changed the angle enough for one child to crawl underneath. The child's mother slid after him, caught his ankle, and passed him up into the watchman's waiting hands on the safe side.

The watchman had crossed without Veer seeing him. Good. Four left on the floating deck now, including Bhanu, who had gone back through to help.

The other child would not crawl. He clung to the waist of the man beside him and screamed whenever the deck moved.

"Mujhe dekho," Veer said.

The child looked at the gate crushing his shoulder instead.

The gangway's outer support slipped off the landing.

Veer's left leg dropped into the river.

Cold struck his hip. His shoulder lost contact with the gate. He caught the lower bar with his right hand, hit the stone guide with his ribs, and felt his left forearm take a wrenching load as he tried to keep his head above the water.

The bar descended another inch.

For a few seconds there was no counted breath, no stance, nothing that could be written in a manual. There was iron in his palm and a boy crying behind it. His shin struck a submerged step. He almost lost the bar in the effort to pull himself back onto the gangway.

Back where he had been.

Again.

He stopped pulling.

Under the bank-side guide, the old stone step continued beneath the water. He could feel its edge with his heel. It was fixed. Not level, not comfortable, but fixed.

Dhruv's voice came from above him, strained now.

"Veer. Main zyada der nahi—"

"Pata hai."

He moved his right hand along the lower bar towards the bank. His ribs scraped the stone. He put the whole sole of his left foot on the submerged step and let himself sink low enough to bend the knee.

There was no room to stand where he had fallen from.

There was room beside it.

Above his head, the gate shuddered in its guides. The next pull would either wrench him loose or take the stonework with it.

Veer turned his shoulder away from the load and opened his empty left hand.

---

His fingers closed around a pale line.

At first Veer thought it was the reflection of the chain on the water. Then the line resisted his grip. A shaft lengthened beneath his palm, straight and hard, its lower end finding the edge of the submerged step.

The upper end pressed against the gate's crossbar.

Not a replacement for the broken baton. Too long, too narrow at the point, balanced along a different purpose. A spear, its light held so closely that the wet iron cast a shadow against it.

Veer could not have lifted it with his left arm alone.

He did not try.

He let the shaft take the angle between gate and stone, brought his right foot into a shallow notch in the bank, and rose from the bent knee.

This time the ground stayed beneath him.

The force entered at the crossbar and travelled down the shaft. He felt it through his shoulder, ribs, hips, the places a fall had taught him to keep separate. Pale bands opened across those places and joined. The shell over his back did not break at the first impact. It shifted, taking the pressure from one joint to the next as his body came upright.

His right hand left the gate.

For a heartbeat he stood without holding himself up.

**"VELUM: SKANDA."**

The skin over his knee tore against the step. Pain struck up his old forearm so fiercely that he nearly dropped the new weapon.

He caught it against his hip instead. The load passed through the broad muscle of his thigh. His arm stayed bent, close to his body, protected without being spared.

The gate rose.

On the floating side, Bhanu stared through the opening.

"Chalo," Veer said.

Bhanu moved at once.

He sent the remaining woman first. She came bent double, dragging one foot. Ira appeared at the bank-side end of the gangway and reached past the watchman to take her elbow.

The man with the frightened child tried to follow too quickly. The deck dipped. The child screamed again and wrapped both legs round his father's waist.

"Mujhe nahi," Veer told him. "Raste ko dekho."

The man lowered his gaze to the strip of gangway beneath the gate.

One foot. Then the other.

The force on Veer's spear increased.

At the launch the handle had stopped turning. The man over the case was pulling a second lever now, short hard strokes with both hands. A thin line ran from the launch into the dark run crossing the water.

The gate twisted towards the river.

Veer's first instinct was to lock his knees. He felt the error start, the old attempt to become a wall because a wall was what stood between people and injury.

He let the knee bend instead.

The spear slid a little along the crossbar, its point biting a new notch into the iron. The pale bands across his back shifted with it. His body went down six inches, not in defeat this time but to keep the path open while the weight changed direction.

The child and his father passed through.

Only Bhanu remained.

The gangway's loose end struck the landing and bounced. Bhanu lost his footing, caught a side rail, and hung for an instant above the water.

Veer took his right hand off the spear.

The shaft wavered. He drove its butt farther into the stone notch and reached towards the wrestler.

Too far.

Bhanu swung a knee over the rail, released one hand, and pushed himself towards the gap. He was rising too, badly, painfully, using everything the akhada had taught him and nothing resembling a beautiful stance.

Veer caught his wrist.

The pull almost tore the weapon free.

He let his own body sink with Bhanu's weight, kept his heel on the fixed step, then came up into the next rise with the wrestler moving beside him. No clean lift. No triumphant straightening. Two men hauled through a narrowing gap, one of them swearing steadily enough for the watchman to know they were both still breathing.

Ira and the watchman took Bhanu's shoulders.

Veer released his wrist.

The floating landing was empty.

"Khaali!" Ira called.

Dhruv looked once along its deck to make certain. Then he let the mooring line go in a controlled spill, turning the angle-ring edgeways against the bollard. The pale light left the wet fibres.

The pontoon swung free of the gangway before it could roll beneath the gate. It drifted towards the shallow inside of the bend, where the watchman's spare rope could catch it later.

Veer remained below the descending iron.

He could hold it. For a little longer he could. The discovery was dizzying: a margin where there had been none, strength not exhausted by its first use.

He nearly spent it proving that the margin existed.

Then he saw Ira on the bank, one hand over the eye nearest the spray, waiting for him to come through rather than staring at what he had become.
