The people were safe. There was no reason to hold the gate.

He turned the spear so its point travelled out of the notch. Kept its butt planted while he moved his right foot onto the next stone. Let the gate descend along the shaft instead of dropping onto his shoulder.

The upper bar scraped past his ear.

He stepped clear.

Iron smashed the empty gangway. The broken planks flew into the river. The counterweight struck the top of its shaft and hung there, chain singing through the wheel.

Veer staggered onto the bank.

The spear remained in his hand for three steps. At the fourth, its point thinned to a line. The joined bands across his body opened at the joints and went out.

His left knee buckled.

Bhanu caught him under the good arm this time. Veer tried to help, discovered that helping required a forearm he could no longer persuade to work, and let himself be lowered against the bollard.

The service launch was moving away.

He could see the metal case beneath a cloth at the man's feet. The short handle had been removed. Something small and bright lay on the launch's gunwale, draining water back into the river.

He had no strength left to follow.

"Jaane do," Dhruv said.

Veer watched the boat pass beyond the old rock.

He was still angry. Saving people had not cured that. It had simply left him in no doubt about where he would have put his feet if he had to choose again.

Beside him, the watchman put a wet hand on the bollard and began saying the names of the people who had crossed.

This time Veer listened without counting over him.

---

At the relief bench, Veer recognized the bandage parcel by Ira's uneven knot.

He laughed when the watchwoman opened it.

"Kya hua?" she asked.

"Advance booking."

She did not find that funny. She cut his trouser leg above the knee and poured water over the grit. The laugh left him in a sharp breath.

The wound was shallow and wide. His ribs were less obliging. Each deep intake found the place where the stone guide had struck him. The watchwoman made him breathe while she pressed carefully along the side; when he tried to turn it into a demonstration of how well he could endure pain, she stopped and waited for him to behave normally.

Ira stood at the end of the bench with a lantern screened towards the wall. The lesson was over; the rescued children needed to see their way up the steps. Even so, the small shaded light looked apologetic beneath Dhruv's sky.

"Left arm," Ira said.

Veer held it out.

His fingers did not close properly.

The old splint had been gone long enough for people to stop asking about it. He had begun to like that silence. Now the watchwoman padded the arm, tied it close to his body, and told him that the doctor would decide in the morning how much damage he had done.

"Toota nahi hai," he said.

"Tumhara kehna note kar liya. Abhi hilao mat."

He stopped arguing.

Below them, Bhanu and the watchman secured the empty pontoon in the inside shallows. Dhruv inspected the mooring line by touch. The rope had left two raw burns across his palm. He wrapped them without mentioning them to Veer.

The people from the landing had gone to the upper court to wait together. Nobody wanted the ferry now. One of the children had fallen asleep sitting upright against his father's chest.

Veer could hear the father speaking softly to him. Not a story. Just the names of things that were still there: bench, lamp, shoulder, shoe.

Veer shut his eyes for a moment.

---

Bhanu brought the recovered fittings up on a board.

The gate's ordinary brake had not failed. A second line had been spliced beneath the covered bearing, where it could pull the counterweight without moving the locked handwheel. Its end ran through a narrow metal eye towards the river. The eye had torn loose when the gate hit the gangway.

"Doosra haath," Bhanu said, showing the splice.

Not a name. A description of the work.

The line led to the place where the launch had waited. Nobody pretended that this proved who had sat in it.

Ira copied the arrangement with ordinary sight. Then she turned the metal eye over.

Its underside was polished, slightly hollow, too clean for a piece that had spent a season beneath a river landing. A tiny circular plate sat inside the hollow. She tilted it towards the lantern, and Veer saw a faint pattern of slots.

"Roof wala?" he asked.

"Uska rishtedaar," she said. "Same nahi bolungi."

She put it beside the drawing rather than against her eye.

Veer appreciated that more than he could explain. It was like watching someone put a weapon down.

"Jo neeche hua," she said after a while, "uska sketch banaungi. Sahi batana."

"Main achha dikhna chahiye."

"Iron ke neeche the. Profile nahi mila."

She drew the fixed step, the gate, the angle of the spear. Her pencil trembled at the long diagonal. She braced the heel of her hand against the board and completed it in short sections.

He corrected one thing. The shaft had not stayed in the first notch. It had moved as he bent his knee.

She erased the single straight arrow and drew several smaller ones.

"Har baar khade nahi hue," she said.

"Hua toh tha."

"Beech mein neeche bhi gaye. Jaan-boojh ke."

Veer watched the new arrows. Down as part of the rise. Not down as a mistake that had to be hidden between two successful positions.

He thought of his manual in its steel tin, the old lesson he had nearly used as proof that there was nothing left to learn.

"Haan," he said. "Woh likhna."

---

Later, when Bhanu went to fetch his bag, Dhruv sat beside him.

The teacher's angle-ring lay across his bandaged palm. One edge had been worn bright by the mooring rope.

Veer nodded towards it. "Aapka bhi equipment gaya."

"Chalega."

"Mera bhi?"

Dhruv glanced at the sling. "Doctor bolega."

Veer looked away first.

From the upper court came the sound of a relieved woman scolding her husband. He had lost one sandal and was proposing to go down in search of it. Her refusal was so complete that even the river seemed to listen.

"Unke peechhe jaa sakta tha," Veer said.

Dhruv did not ask whom he meant.

"Jaate toh yahan kaun rehta?"

"Aap."

The teacher lifted his rope-burned hand. Not to shame him. Simply to include it in the account.

Veer nodded.

"Dono jagah nahi tha main," Dhruv said. "Tum bhi nahi the. Isliye log ek se zyada hote hain."

For a while neither spoke.

Then Veer asked for a scrap of paper. With his good hand he wrote a note to put inside the tin, separate from the old handwriting.

*Uthna aata hai. Ab jagah chunni hai.*

He knew how to rise. Now he had to choose where.

He let the paper dry before folding it. Some things did not improve by being hurried into a pocket.

---

Ira stayed behind while the watchwoman arranged a slow walk home for him. Bhanu would go on his good side. There would be no celebratory run through the lanes.

She wanted one more ordinary look at the metal eye before Vedant collected it.

The board was wet. A shallow basin beneath the bench held the water left from cleaning Veer's knee. In it the lantern made a narrow trembling road.

Ira lifted the fitting by its unpolished rim.

For an instant the wet hollow reflected Veer's pale spear exactly as she had seen it beneath the gate. She knew it was gone; she had watched it go. She lowered the fitting and waited for the afterimage to fade.

It faded.

Good. She wrote *afterimage — unconfirmed* beside the sketch.

Then she noticed the little plate inside the hollow had turned.

The lantern had not moved. Neither had the board. She shifted her face to the right, and the plate followed a fraction behind, its slots gathering into a dark point at the centre of her reflected eye.

She stopped breathing for a moment.

From the steps Veer called, "Ira?"

She set the fitting down with its polished side against the wood.

The plate clicked once underneath.

She had turned it towards the light to examine it.

It had taken the angle of her eye instead.

---

---

# CHAPTER FIFTY-FOUR — THE MIRROR THAT COUNTED BACK

---

> *"First description, struck out: I became as strong as the person opposite me.*
> *Correction: I returned what reached me. When the source stopped, so did I.*
> *The distinction was the part we could least afford to learn late."*
>
> *— Ira Mishra, loose sheet later sewn into the field register*

---

The morning after the landing failed, Ira drew her own eye and put a line through it.

Not the eye. The arrow pointing out of it.

She had drawn the instrument as something being inspected, the way she would draw a damaged hinge. Last night its little plate had turned towards the angle of her gaze. Her diagram contained no arrow for what had come back.

She added one.

The two arrowheads met in the narrow space between the pencilled eye and the polished fitting. Her hand shook enough to make the meeting look accidental.

Across the kitchen, Veer tried to lower himself onto a chair without admitting that his knee hurt. The doctor's film had shown no fresh break in his forearm. That was the good news. The doctor's instructions about rest, strain and the sling had taken longer to repeat.

Ira waited until he was seated before moving his tea closer.

"Meri aankh itni badi hai?" he asked, looking at the drawing.

"Tumhari nahi."

"Phir theek hai."

He picked up the cup with his right hand. His left stayed where the doctor had put it.

Aadhya sat beside the open door with her palms resting on folded cloth. The right thumb and index finger were still numb. She could spread the other fingers, slowly. A little pile of clean dressings lay beside her; no one had suggested that the new form made dressings unnecessary.

Rudra came in carrying bread. He set it down without bringing his hands near the covered instrument on the table.

Ira noticed that too.

They ate before trying anything. Rukmini made certain of it. She had packed an extra tiffin for the aid post above Manikarnika, where the night watch had run out of clean cloth and patience in approximately equal measure.

"Aap wahan jaa rahi ho?" Rudra asked.

"Dopahar tak. Upar wala kamra. Ram saath jaayenge."

"Kal roof pe—"

"Isliye aaj bina bataye nahi jaa rahi."

She did not soften the sentence. Neither did she refuse the escort or the inspection Vedant had insisted on. Her work would happen in a room they had checked, above a route they could clear.

Ira watched Rudra accept a plan that was not the same as keeping his mother indoors. She was glad not to be the one who had to make that choice.

Rukmini tied the tiffin in a square of cloth. Before handing it over, she put a second packet beside Ira's maps.

"Tumhare liye."

"Main sirf fittings dekhne—"

"Khaana fittings se alag rakhna. Tel lag jaata hai."

Ira moved the packet to the dry side of her bag.

On its paper cover, in Rukmini's broad hand, was her name. Nothing else was required to say it had been packed for somebody expected to return.

---

They tested the metal eye in the courtyard with a wall between it and the street.

Vedant set it on a brick. He did not open the little plate. Sitara sat beside Ira, while Veer watched from a chair with his bandaged leg stretched out. Rudra stayed by the door; he had agreed not to offer the seam as a test source.

First Ira looked at the fitting without reaching through her sight.

The plate moved slightly. Not enough to prove it had followed her. Vedant marked its position in chalk.

Next he passed a hand between it and her face. No change.

Then she let the courtyard's edges loosen, the small act of attention that usually showed where a movement was about to go.

The plate turned a clear fraction.

She stopped. It stopped.

"Roshni nahi," she said.

"Sirf roshni nahi," Vedant corrected.

He covered the polished hollow with cloth. This time the motion was slower, but it did not cease until he put the whole fitting in a clay bowl and filled the hollow with wet sand.

Ira felt a faint release behind her eyes.

The thing had not been looking at her face in the ordinary sense. That much they could write down.

She wrote it, carefully leaving out the rest.

"Kal spear kyun dikha?" Veer asked.

"Us waqt tumhara tha. Main dekh rahi thi. Baad wala image rukha nahi."

"Toh mera copy nahi?"

"Proof nahi."

He nodded. No disappointment, no urging her to try harder. He had learned what the empty space after *proof* could cost.

Sitara slid a plain steel plate in front of Ira. Its worn surface reflected the neem above them in broken patches.

"Isme kya hai?"

"Tree."

"Tree nikalo."

Ira looked at her.

"Nahi nikal sakti," Sitara agreed. "Par roshni ki jagah badal sakti ho. Bina tree hue."

She tipped the plate. The reflected patch moved across the wall.

It was a demonstration, not an explanation of Ira's blood or dreams. She was grateful for that. Too many things in her life seemed to wait for an origin before anybody would let them have a present use.

Veer rested his good elbow on the chair's arm.

"Chhota shield," he said. "Haath nahi uthaunga."

"Pain hua toh band," Ira told him.

"Tum bhi."

A pale curve showed above his right palm, no wider than a tea saucer. Ira looked at it, then at the broken reflection in the steel.

Two curves appeared.

One belonged to Veer.

She could not yet tell whether the other belonged anywhere at all.

---

The second curve vanished when Veer closed his hand.

Ira kept looking. Nothing remained for her to hold or call back. When he opened his palm again, the faint line reappeared a little to the left of the first.

She put a loose thread against it.

The thread fell through.

Image, not force. Not yet.

She tried once more, this time watching the place where the line began instead of the outline she wanted to complete. Pressure gathered behind her nose. The courtyard doubled at its far edge.

She closed her eyes before the snap could come.

"Band," she said.

Veer's pale curve went out at once.

Neither asked for another attempt.

Sitara took the steel plate away. "Jo tumhari taraf aata hai, usko jagah de rahi ho. Jo banaana chahti ho, usko nahi. Farq hai."

Ira washed her face in the courtyard water. She could understand the distinction on paper. Her eyes still wanted to invent a whole shape from the promise of one.

She wrote three observations without using the word *success*.

*Live source required. Image shifts with angle. No contact confirmed.*

Below them she left room for the observation she did not yet have.

---

They went to the ghat after the morning rush had thinned.

Veer rode with the recovered gate fittings on the supply handcart. His bandaged knee rested on a rolled sack, and the doctor would have recognized no part of the arrangement as an excuse for exercise. Ira walked beside the cart. Sitara carried the bowl in which the polished eye lay packed with wet sand.

The air changed before they reached the main steps. Woodsmoke settled low between the houses. A priest rinsed ash from a brass spoon. Two men rested a bier on their knees while another cleared the way ahead, speaking quietly rather than shouting. Ira folded her map until there was room for them to pass.

Nothing she was looking for had the right to make these people background.

At the upper aid room, Ram had checked the window brackets, the floor and the passage behind the grain bins. Rudra was helping a volunteer mark the longer evacuation route with lengths of white cloth. He had moved the marks high enough to remain visible above a crowd.

Rukmini arrived with the tiffin and clean dressings. She put the food where the night attendants could find it without asking, then began replacing the wicks in the room's small lamps. The windows had to remain partly shuttered against sparks from below.

Ira set her own food packet beside the maps, still unopened.

Rukmini looked at it once.

"Baad mein," Ira said.

"Main bhi yahi bolke nikli thi."

She left half a roti on a clean square of paper beside Ira's hand. Ira ate it while reading the riverwatch list.

Three fittings had been found since dawn. Two were ordinary gauge collars, badly installed in the hurried repairs. The third sat in a shallow niche below the terrace, its polished face turned away from the river.

Towards the people on the stairs.

Vedant was already beside it, standing clear of its front. He had wedged a wooden board between its bracket and the stone rather than let a workman pull it loose.

"Neeche aur line hai," he told Ira. "Upar ka kholenge toh doosri taraf load jaayega. Pehle jagah khaali."

Two volunteers began moving traffic to the far stair. Sitara stayed with them. Ram took Rukmini back into the upper room while Rudra cleared the passage beside it.

Ira knelt on the safe side of the niche.

The gauge face contained two apertures. One was dark. In the other, light moved over a narrow polished scale.

She looked without reaching.

Nothing.

She let her sight find the pressure beneath the bracket.

The dark aperture brightened.

It was not gathering force from the stone. A thin trace came in from farther downriver, passed through the fitting, and went out again along a different angle. She followed the outgoing trace until it touched her own position on the terrace.

There it stopped being a line she could observe from outside.

A little pressure touched the back of her left eye.

She withdrew.

The aperture darkened at once.

"Wapas jaata hai," she said.

Vedant kept his hands still. "Kya?"

"Hum dekhte hain. Woh dekhna naapta hai. Phir wapas bhejta hai."

She drew two arrows in the dust. One out. One back. Her finger shook between them.

The roof fitting, the drain tongue, the eye beneath the gate: she could not prove every connection yet. But the same mistake ran through their assumptions. They had treated the instruments as passive witnesses.

Each time the team answered one, the answer went somewhere.

Vedant filled the bright aperture with wet sand from the bowl. Its light ceased.

Farther down the stairs, beneath a stack of empty wicker baskets, something clicked in reply.

He looked at Ira.

"Aur hain."

---

The second fitting did not wait for them to inspect it.

A low note pressed against Ira's teeth. Dust jumped out of the seam between two steps. The wooden board over the first bracket split down its middle.

Veer turned on the handcart.

"Neeche mat utro," Ira said.

He stayed seated, swung his good hand towards the stairs, and opened a protective curve above his palm.

A line of compressed air struck it.

The curve flattened. His right elbow drove into the cart rail. The impact made him gasp against the bruised ribs.

The people on the far stair stopped. Then those behind them, unable to see why, took another step forward.

Sitara lifted both arms to halt the upper crowd. Rudra moved along the white-cloth route, making space without touching his seam. The mark beneath his sleeve was bright enough for Ira to see through the cloth.

Another low note sounded.

This time the dust jumped closer to Veer's cart.

The aperture had changed angle after meeting his shield.

It was correcting for the answer.

Ira looked at the pale curve above his good hand. In her loosened sight a second curve appeared, clear enough to show the little gap where his concentration was failing.

She could see it.

The thread in the courtyard had fallen through.

Now there was no time to test with a thread.

---

Ira tried to move the second curve to the foot of the stairs.

It came apart before it reached the stone.

She had asked it to exist where she wanted it. The source was still over Veer's hand. Between the two places there was nothing but her certainty that a reflection ought to be able to move.

The fitting sounded again.

A strip of plaster flew off the cart rail. Veer pulled his good hand in by an inch, caught the pressure on his curve, and kept it from reaching his chest.

"Aur kitna?" Ira asked.

"Pata nahi. Jaldi."

That was better than a lie. She could work with it.

The lower stairs offered three possible lines in her second sight. On one, the pulse went past the cart into the wall. On another, Veer turned his wrist too late. On the third, it broke across the place where the people were trying to descend.

She could spend the remaining seconds choosing which future she liked.

Or look at what was actually there.

She let the three lines go.

The real curve above Veer's hand shook. Its pressure touched her sight, and with it came an unpleasant awareness of her own position: face turned towards the stairs, left eye nearest the bright aperture, shoulder within the return angle.

She had been leaving herself out of every diagram.

"Mujhe bhi gin raha hai," she said.

Vedant heard. "Peechhe ho jaao."

If she stepped back, she would be safer. The fitting would continue correcting against Veer's defence until it found the gap. If she closed her sight altogether, she would lose the thin line that showed where its return entered the niche.

She lowered herself behind the stone lip, keeping only her eyes above it.

"Main iska raasta dekh sakti hoon."

"Ira—"

"Apna bhi."

She turned her palm towards Veer, not towards the stair she wanted to save. The faint second curve returned, close to the first. This time she did not drag it away.

She received its edge.

The pressure was smaller than she expected. Exact. A narrow piece of somebody else's work arriving through the place where her sight met it. Beyond that edge, nothing belonged to her: not Veer's strength, not his weapon, not the margin he had found under the gate.

Only this contact, while he maintained it.

She shifted her own angle instead of moving the image by force.

The second curve turned.

A dropped chip of plaster struck it and bounced.

Ira drew breath. The blurred rim of the world sharpened into two clean faces, one facing the source and one facing away. Fine silver lines crossed her irises. She could feel a flatness opening beyond her palm, no thicker than the instant in which an image becomes recognizable.

**"DRISHTI: DARPAN."**

Blood warmed the lower lid of her left eye. A dark blur spread over that side of the ghat, swallowing half the stair she had been trying to see.

She kept the right eye open.

The reflected curve held.

Its strength did not feel like Veer's whole body entering hers. It felt like the curve itself: limited, trembling, with the same weak gap near its base. She could not erase that gap by wanting a better version.

But she could choose where the gap faced.

Ira turned her wrist a fraction.

The reflected shield appeared against the inside rim of the lower fitting, its convex face pointing into the polished hollow. Exactly the size of Veer's small curve. Large enough to cover the aperture. No larger.

The next note struck it from within.

Veer gasped. The source shivered above his hand. Ira's reflection shivered with it, and the pressure that should have crossed the stairs drove sideways into the brass bracket instead.

One of its screws pulled out of the stone.

"Left," Veer said.

She could no longer see what he meant. The blur had spread to the edge of her right eye's field, taking depth with it.

"Tumhara left?"

"Fitting ka. Neeche. Chhota."

She moved the angle a little.

"Haan. Wahi."

Another screw lifted. The niche rang. A long split opened along the lower plate.

The fitting turned its dark aperture towards her.

She felt the return touch behind her eyes with horrible precision, no longer a general pressure but an attempt to find the exact place from which she was looking.

Closing her sight would release it.

Closing her sight would also release the curve inside the fitting before the last fixing tore.

She stayed.

"Padh lo," she whispered. "Is baar main bhi dekh rahi hoon."

The dark trace leading downriver brightened in her one clear eye. There was a junction beneath the next landing, and another beyond it. Neither was the beginning. The line continued towards the bend below Manikarnika, where three vertical spars stood under tarpaulin beside a service stair.

She did not follow it farther. One glance was already more than she could afford.

"Neeche landing. Tarpaulin. Teen khambe," she told Vedant.

He repeated the words back exactly, so she would not have to spend breath making certain he had heard.

At the cart, Veer's right hand began to close.

Her reflected curve thinned.

"Bas," she said.

He let go.

The reflection vanished at once.

The fitting discharged into its own half-torn bracket. Brass struck the empty step below. The note broke into a ragged cough, and the pressure on the crowded stair ceased.

Sitara moved the first waiting people through the white-cloth passage. Rudra stayed at its narrow turn, one hand against the wall, telling them where the next step was. He did not look at the heat under his sleeve. He looked at their feet.

Ira tried to rise.

The ghat tilted in a way that had nothing to do with second sight.

Vedant caught her elbow and put her hand against the stone lip. "Baithi raho."

"Wapas gaya," she said.

"Pehle saans."

"Mera dekhna. Wapas gaya."

She needed him to understand the cost before relief made them call it a clean victory. The instrument had broken. Its reading had left before it broke.

Her right hand trembled too hard to keep the fingers on the same patch of stone.

Vedant folded a clean cloth and gave it to her. "Aankh pe dabana nahi. Bas neeche rakho."

She held it beneath the wet left lid.

A red crescent appeared in the cloth.

Across the terrace Veer was still seated on the cart. His right elbow bled where it had hit the rail. He opened his hand once and found no curve left above it.

Their eyes met.

"Source band," he said, because it was a fact he could offer her without promising anything else.

Ira nodded.

The space beyond her palm was empty again. She could not call his shield back. She could not replace the portion of the stair hidden behind the blur.

She could hear the people getting through.

For now she let that be enough.

---

The aid-room attendant made Ira cover the left eye without pressing on it.

The cloth turned the world into a flat arrangement of edges. She could see with the right. Judging how far away a cup stood was another matter. Twice she reached short of it before Rukmini moved it into her hand.

"Abhi padhna nahi," the attendant said.

Ira nodded.

"Likho bhi mat."

She lowered the pencil.

The attendant made her practise finding the chair arm with the back of her hand before putting weight on it. On stairs she would need someone to name the edge aloud. Ira disliked the instruction enough to repeat it back exactly; if she was going to hate a limit, she would at least remember where it was.

Veer sat opposite her with a fresh dressing on his right elbow and the old sling still holding the left arm. Between them lay a blank sheet that neither could make useful by stubbornness.

"Main bolungi," Ira said. "Tum yaad rakhna."

"Isko treatment bolte hain ya punishment?"

She might have laughed if the change in pressure had not hurt behind her covered eye.

He leaned forward at once. "Sorry."

"Joke kharaab tha. Injury alag hai."

He settled back, reassured by the distinction.

She dictated what had happened in plain words. The reflected power had come from a live source. It had kept the source's size, weakness and interruption. She had changed its place by changing the return angle. When Veer stopped, the reflection had stopped, with nothing left to summon.

"Ek waqt pe ek," he said.

"Abhi utna hi pata hai. Zyada claim mat karna."

He repeated the observations until she was satisfied he had not improved them into a legend.

Then he added the part she had skipped.

"Tumhara reading chala gaya."

Ira sat very still.

"Haan."

He did not tell her it was worth it. The people had got through. The enemy had received something. Both belonged on the page.

Rukmini put the unopened food packet between them. This time Ira let Veer tear the paper while she held it down.

---

The second mark was found where Rukmini had been standing.

Not a wound. A narrow black stroke on the limewashed window recess, level with the knot on her wick bundle. The wood beside it was warm although the lamps had been moved away.

Ram brought the bundle into the light. One outer reed had been cut almost through. Rukmini had not felt it happen.

Rudra's face changed.

"Aapko—"

"Nahi," she said before he could finish. She showed him both hands, turned them, opened the fingers. Uninjured.

He looked anyway.

Vedant asked her to leave the bundle on the sill and move back. In the broken fitting below, a small sighting tongue shifted towards the empty space beside the aid-room door.

Rukmini stood there now.

They moved the bundle again. The tongue did not follow it.

Ram stepped between the niche and the door.

"Andar," he said.

This time Rukmini went at once.

They transferred the remaining patients to the room behind the grain store, one by one. It had no river-facing window. A watchman stayed at its street door, and the white-cloth route was moved to match the new exit.

Ira heard the work from her bench. She did not remove the eye dressing to confirm it herself. Other people could observe things. She had spent too much of her life behaving as though everything unobserved by her would disappear.

When Ram returned, she asked only, "Rukmini-ji theek?"

He nodded, then moved her cup precisely against her good hand before going back to the door.

At the far end of the bench Rudra rested his forehead briefly against the wall. His hand stayed open. No tapping.

---

By late afternoon the physical evidence lay on one table: roof-washer sketch, peeled drain tongue, gate splice, broken niche plate. The polished eye remained packed in wet sand.

Ira dictated from memory. Vedant drew the joins. He used different marks for what she had seen and what he inferred, and did not connect a broken line merely because it would make a more satisfying diagram.

The returns ran downriver towards the covered spars. Other lines fed them from the city side. The ordinary service stair above the landing had become a place through which too many separate measurements passed.

"Wahi main machine hai?" Rudra asked.

"Wahi ek jagah hai jahan hum dekhne jaayenge," Vedant said. "Dono ko ek mat samjho."

"Aaj?"

"Bina sight khole. Bina naam bulaaye. Pairon se."

He sent two experienced watchmen along the public lane, with instructions to come back rather than make an arrest. Ram would walk the upper route. There were still families at the ghat who could not be moved simply by writing CLOSED on a board.

Ira wanted to go. Her body made the refusal before anyone else had to. When she stood, the table edge slid away from her hand and she sat again too hard.

Sitara moved the chair closer to the wall.

"Aaj jo dekha woh sambhaal lo," she said. "Baaki hum dekh lenge."

Ira rested her good eye. Behind the covered one the stair still seemed to be there, with a dark aperture at its foot. She knew the image was not proof of continuing sight. She named five sounds in the room until the false depth receded.

Then she asked for her unfinished breakfast drawing.

Vedant brought it. She dictated a correction to the arrows: one line for the power received, one for the power returned, and a separate mark for what the instrument had learned about the receiver.

"Mera naam mat likhna," she said.

"Kyun?"

"Rule hai. Sabke liye."

He wrote RECEIVER beside the mark.

The diagram looked less like a portrait. That made it more frightening.

---

At evening the watchmen came back with dust on their knees.

There were three covered spars, as Ira had said. Between them, workmen had fitted an iron frame round an ordinary service winch. No public works order named the contractor. The old waterline went beneath it; fresh cables had been laid alongside, concealed under the stair's replacement boards.

The watchmen had not touched them.

"Kal tak nahi the," one said.

The other shook his head. "Cover tha. Humne kal dekha nahi. Alag baat."

Ira opened her good eye. "Wahi likhna."

Rudra listened to the report standing beside the new patient-room door. Beyond it, his mother was helping a woman change her child's dressing. The familiar low voice reached him clearly through the wood.

He stayed where he could hear it.

Far downriver, in a portable instrument case, a strip of paper advanced past a brass stylus. Three short traces crossed the strip. A fourth remained broken.

An attendant cut the paper, fed its measurements into a punch, and laid the card in the Right Hand's dispatch tray.

**LAUTNE KA PATA MILA.**

The return address has been found.

Beneath the line, a second reference mark had been added in pencil: not the eye, not the shield, not the roots.

A small lamp, beside a doorway.

---

---

# CHAPTER FIFTY-FIVE — TWO ARITHMETICS

---

> *"Three names were available for the first evacuation bulletin. The fourth space was left blank.*
> *Later copies call this an omission. The original clerk's correction reads: He was there. Do not replace a man with the name of a thing he had not become."*
>
> *— Manikarnika night orders, surviving carbon copy*

---

By evening Rudra knew exactly which door his mother was behind.

He had checked the rear grain-store room, walked its street exit, and counted the steps between the patient benches and the corner where a stretcher would have to turn. There were two watchmen outside. The river-facing room stood empty, its lamps moved and its shutters closed.

None of this changed the little black mark on the window recess.

When he shut his eyes, he saw it at the height of her wrist.

Rukmini sat beside the last patient who could not yet walk. The woman had been waiting for a cart with space to keep her injured leg straight. Her son slept on the floor beneath the bench with a rolled shawl under his head.

Rudra brought a new pillow from the upper room. His mother took it, eased it behind the woman's back, and only then looked at the open collar of his shirt.

"Dhaaga nikla hai," she said.

He looked down. A loose thread lay beside the new seam-line towards his collarbone.

"Kapde ka," she added.

"Pata hai."

She held out her hand. He bent so she could trim the thread without pulling on the skin beneath.

For a few seconds her wrist was inches from his face. He could see the faint marks left by carrying hot pots, a little crescent of flour she had missed when washing, the place where a bangle had rubbed the skin smooth. The black line on the window had been aimed at this ordinary, irreplaceable piece of her.

She cut the thread. Her free hand settled briefly against his chest, feeling the heartbeat through cotton. He did not look at her until she took it away.

"Main akhada jaa raha hoon," he said.

Her scissors stopped over the cloth.

"Sab honge. Ek release drill. Bas."

She studied him, then looked towards the patient's sleeping son.

"Wapas aake seedha yahin aana. Khana rakhungi."

He wanted her to tell him not to go. He wanted to be furious if she did.

Instead he nodded.

At the door Ram waited with his upper-arm bandage showing beneath the sleeve. A watchman had taken his place inside the room. The late cart was coming by the landward lane, not along the river steps.

Rudra repeated the route to himself once before leaving.

---

They did not call it a transformation lesson.

Rudra had asked to try one. Ram had refused. So had Vedant.

The force was arriving without permission now, sometimes when Rudra slept, sometimes when he heard a sound that reminded him of the pit. Simply forbidding its use would not teach him to release it when his hands stopped obeying. That was the work they had agreed to do.

One opening. One release. No extra breath to see whether the next attempt would be better.

If his whole body found a shape it could hold, he would not chase the shape beyond the agreed limit.

The paath-shala had been cleared. Its split stool lay outside, marked with chalk so nobody would carry it back in. The damaged plaster had been removed from the eastern wall. Above it the four whitewashed names remained.

Vedant had checked the ground and the roof, then set wet clay along the known service holes. He did not claim this made them invisible to the instruments. It removed the fittings they could identify; the readings already sent could not be taken back.

Dhruv stood at the outer door, measuring distance to the empty yard. The gathering was not a sky lesson. He had come because someone would have to keep the exit clear if the water or the floor moved.

Veer sat on a low charpai with the injured knee supported and his left arm slung. A short timber brace lay beside his good hand. His job was to knock the basin away if the water began standing up again, not hold anything for Rudra.

Ira sat beyond the marked boundary, left eye covered. She would watch the release with ordinary sight. Sitara sat beside her, keeping the path to the door free.

Aadhya had both wrapped palms on her knees. She had brought no plant to use as a test.

Nobody was being asked to repeat a new form for the sake of the exercise.

Rudra looked at them. The sling, the eye dressing, the thick cloth beneath Aadhya's fingers. A few days earlier he would have seen a team that needed him to be stronger.

Tonight he saw people who ought to be resting.

"Hum postpone kar sakte hain," he said.

Ram set the shallow basin on a sound block.

"Tumhari marzi se shuru. Tumhare signal pe band. Aaj nahi toh nahi."

The words cost his father something. Rudra could hear the effort of making them true, rather than merely saying them.

He put his bare feet on the sand. Tested the heel scrape twice.

Every face turned at the sound.

That helped.

"Ek baar," he said.

Vedant moved a little water into the basin. No tilt this time. No load to overcome. Only a visible surface that would tell them when something changed.

Rudra opened his right hand.

Nine breaths remained the allowance he knew. The four-cadence had not become less persuasive because he understood that it was foreign to his choice.

He did not try to take thirty-six breaths or find a clever interval where the numerals shared a mark. They were not sums waiting to be solved. They were demands made on the same chest.

He took the first breath slowly.

Gold warmed beneath the seam.

The water stayed flat.

---

The second breath was supposed to be the release.

Rudra opened his fingers wider. The gold dimmed. Water trembled once against the basin's edge.

Then his chest took a third breath without waiting for him.

"Abhi band," Ram said.

Rudra tried.

The four arrived whole, no longer a count he heard approaching. His shoulders settled into its short hard rhythm. The next intake cut across the unfinished exhalation, and the seam pulled tight beneath his ribs.

Gold ran towards the breastbone in fine, ordered branches. Red-black pressure climbed the other way, from the clenched heat in his palm. Each had enough force to shape him. Neither would make room for the other.

For a terrible instant he could feel the outline of a whole body beyond his ordinary one: a shoulder that would not yield, a hand large with pressure, feet that could have driven through stone.

The outline split down its centre before it held.

His left side tried to turn. His right remained fixed. Pain passed through the join like a saw taking the wrong grain.

He scraped his heel twice.

Ram moved. Veer knocked the basin off its block with the timber. Vedant took the falling water into his own basin before it could stand upright between them again.

The force did not need the water.

Sand rose round Rudra's ankles in a thin ring. The eastern wall clicked as something inside the plaster took load. A fresh line opened in the gold beneath his collar, running higher than the one left by the first basin drill.

"Sab bahar," Ram said.

Rudra could hear him. That was the worst part. He was not lost inside a fury that would spare him the memory. He could see Ira reaching for the chair arm with the back of her hand as instructed. Veer trying not to put weight on the bad knee. Aadhya rising with both injured hands held away from her sides.

They were leaving because of him.

The pressure offered him an easy correction: hold the outline still, prove they did not need to run. Four short closures. The nine-breath allowance of the seal demanded the opposite, a long slow allowance his lungs could no longer finish.

Neither count was asking what the room could survive.

Inside the held bell, a thin workshop sound passed through the wall. A tool being set down. The old voice, dry as filings:

*Naap badlega toh lakdi apne aap nahi badlegi.*

He did not ask it to explain.

Ram's hand reached his shoulder.

The contact almost worked. Rudra found the weight, the cloth beneath it, the familiar pressure of an ordinary palm. His feet returned to him for half a heartbeat.

Then the split outline threw its shoulder backwards.

Ram struck the wall beside the door. A beam shifted above him. He caught its edge with one hand, saving his head, and the movement opened a shallow cut across his palm.

The sight made the red-black pressure surge.

Rudra could have called it anger at himself. He recognized the uglier possibility beneath that excuse: anger at the hand that had interrupted him. It was small, cold and real enough to frighten him more than the pain.

He would not give it his father's face.

"Bahar," he managed. "Please."

The beam dropped another inch.

Aadhya put her wrapped palms against the earth just outside the door. Green branches lit her wrists, then her arms. She did not reach into Rudra. She found the living root beside the foundation and gave the sinking doorpost a wider patch of ground to bear on.

Blood soaked through the cloth under her index finger.

The post held.

Veer rose only far enough to put his good foot beneath him. A pale spear formed against the timber brace; he set it under the slipping beam, its butt angled towards the sound block beside the door. Joined bands crossed his chest. His injured knee shook violently.

"Ram-ji. Neeche se."

Ram ducked clear.

A narrow protective curve opened along Veer's right forearm, catching the first spray of plaster. It could not cover the gap beside Ira's chair.

Ira turned her uncovered eye towards that curve.

"Do second," she said.

The reflected edge appeared in the gap. No larger than the source. No second layer. It caught a sharp piece of limewash that would have struck Sitara's neck.

The cloth over Ira's left eye darkened at its lower edge.

Sitara pulled her through the doorway. The reflected curve vanished as soon as Veer let his own go.

None of it changed the war inside Rudra's chest.

They had protected an exit. They had not joined the two forces, repaired the seam or given him a name he could borrow.

Dhruv was in the yard, keeping the fleeing sand from burying the threshold. Thin light ran from his angle-ring to the stone at his feet. Beyond him the empty ground waited where the students had been told to leave their bags.

Rudra looked at that emptiness.

He had been trying, even now, to release the force while keeping the shape. To let everyone see him stop successfully. To emerge standing, because collapsing would make the attempt another failure in a book already full of them.

The split outline tightened round his jaw.

He let the failure happen.

Not another count. Not a new arrangement of four and nine. He stopped holding the shoulder in place and let his knees go where they had been trying to go since the first pain.

The outline broke.

Pressure struck the empty yard in a flat red-black sheet, crossed by gold lines that snapped before they could form a pattern. Dhruv turned the last people behind the stone wall. The clay seals burst out of the service holes. Dust rolled over the abandoned bags.

The eastern plaster split from floor to lintel.

Rudra landed on his knees in ordinary sand.

There was no larger hand round his own. No altered body waiting when the light cleared. Only his fingers, scraped raw from closing too hard, and the hot new seam rising to the base of his neck.

For a while he could not lift his head.

Then he heard someone breathing close to him. Ram, crouched just outside arm's reach. Not touching until he knew the work had stopped.

"Main hoon," Rudra said.

It sounded too small after the noise.

Ram nodded as if it were the answer he had been waiting for.

Veer lowered the beam onto the brace. Aadhya took her palms off the ground, one edge at a time. Her green faded before she sat. Ira stayed beyond the doorway with Sitara, eye covered, calling the place where the floor ended so nobody would trip.

The four names on the wall were still legible. A crack passed through the space between them.

Rudra tried to stand and found that he could not.

This time he did not pretend the attempt was anything else.

---

They got him onto the charpai after Veer had moved to a chair.

The exchange took longer than the failed drill. Someone had to hold Veer's bad knee clear. Someone had to tell Ira where the stool stood. Aadhya could not grip the water cup, so Sitara held it while she drank.

Rudra watched the care pass from one person to another without ever becoming effortless.

He had imagined a completed form as something that would end this part: the carrying, the dressings, the humiliating need to be told where to put a foot. He could see how foolish that hope had been, spread round him in three different kinds of bandage.

Ram washed the shallow cut in his palm. The older upper-arm dressing had held. He flexed the fingers once, found them usable, and reached for a clean strip.

"Main kar doon?" Rudra asked.

Ram looked at his scraped hands.

"Bolte rehna. Main baandh lunga."

So Rudra told him when the loose end slipped beneath the knot. An ordinary task. It let him keep looking at his father's hand without having to apologize every time.

When the bandage was secure, he asked, "Pata tha aisa hoga?"

Ram did not pretend to misunderstand.

"Pata tha mushkil hoga. Aaj jaisa hoga, nahi."

"Aur agar main rukta nahi?"

His father sat on the edge of the empty block.

"Tab humein aur jaldi nikalna padta."

It was the first answer that did not make Rudra's stopping inevitable. They had had a plan. It might not have been enough. His father could admit that and still sit there rather than reach for another drill.

"Aapne rukna kab sikhaya tha?" Rudra asked.

Ram looked at the cracked wall. Not away from him exactly. Towards something both of them could see.

"Kam sikhaya," he said.

The admission did not mend nineteen years. Rudra had not expected it to. He lay back slowly, protecting the new hot line at the base of his neck, and let the words remain unfinished between them.

---

The Load Khata was dusty but intact.

He dictated because his hands shook when he tried to hold the pencil. Sitara wrote without choosing better words for him.

*No form held.*

*Removing the water did not stop the force.*

*Their forms kept the exit open. They could not breathe for me.*

After a pause he added:

*I tried to keep the outline while stopping the work. That kept the work going.*

Sitara read it back.

He nodded. It was not a solution. It was at least a more exact failure than *not strong enough*.

Across the room Veer sat with his right hand on the timber brace. His new spear was gone. Aadhya's green had left the root under the doorway and returned the load to the mason's ordinary foundation. Ira kept her eye covered; the dressing would have to be changed again before they moved.

"Aaj aur nahi," Rudra said.

Nobody argued him into hope.

Then Aadhya asked for the khata.

Sitara held it while she pointed with the knuckle of her uninjured little finger.

"Yeh bhi. Tumne signal diya. Humne suna."

He looked at the heel-marks in the sand.

The drill had not ended safely enough. But he had not had to vanish behind a wall of water for them to know he needed it stopped.

He allowed the sentence into the account.

---

Outside, the district had begun making a story from incomplete reports.

At the tea stall somebody said three names had been heard in three days. Someone else supplied a fourth, invented from the colour of the dust. The mason, on his way to inspect the paath-shala, told him to stop making the injured do extra work in his mouth.

A boy carrying a diversion notice asked what he should write in the fourth space.

"Rudra," Veer said from the doorway.

The boy waited for more.

"Naam poochha tha na. Wahi hai."

He let the boy go with that.

Rudra heard him from the charpai. He did not call him back to turn the kindness into a joke.

At the chauk, the keeper's former assistant received the evening slips. The green-thread ledger lay open before her, the first unfamiliar entry still untouched: *Aath. — Chal raha hai.* She added the actual road closures on a separate page. Nobody had given the retired keeper his office back merely because the night was difficult.

The middle lamp at the haveli burned behind a screened window. Its place had not been reassigned to a new title. Sitara had left instructions for it to be tended while she was away.

Small continuities. Enough for the district to recognize its own evening while its roads changed beneath it.

Then the pump handles stopped.

Not all at once. The western one first, then the schoolyard pump, then the one whose pipe ran towards the old service stair by Manikarnika.

Vedant heard the reports and set water in his basin. Its surface drew towards the river side although the basin stood level.

"Drill ka bacha hua?" Ram asked.

Vedant shook his head. "Yahan se nahi. Udhar se kheench raha hai."

The cracked processional bell made no sound in the air. Beneath the paath-shala floor, however, Rudra felt a low vibration settle through the stone and remain. The bell's old downward grammar, reaching them by ground and water rather than a ringing mouth.

A watchman came into the yard, out of breath.

The late patient cart had been turned back at the service lane. Men with no public-works order were unrolling cable across it. The rear room's landward door remained clear for people on foot, but no stretcher could make the narrow turn without the cart.

Rukmini had sent a message with him.

*Patient yahin hai. Main bhi. Raasta pehle khulwao.*

The patient is here. So am I. Open the way first.

Rudra sat up before the watchman finished.

This time he asked where the sound ground was before putting his feet down.

---

They went back for the patient, not to give the new forms a battlefield.

Vedant said it twice. Once in the yard, once at the first lane junction, where three volunteers offered to bring axes for the cable.

"Load kahan jaayega, pehle woh dekhenge," he told them. "Kaatna baad mein."

Dhruv went ahead with the watchman. Veer rode the narrow supply cart, his knee raised; he could get down at the tight corners, unlike the patient whose whole injured leg needed a flat bed. Sitara walked on Ira's covered side and named each step before they reached it. Aadhya held the cart's side with her left palm, not the numb fingers of her right.

Rudra carried the two light wooden rails they would need if the patient had to be moved off the waiting stretcher. He could still do that much.

His collar rubbed the new seam at the base of his neck. He loosened it and left the mark visible.

The night district made room for them. Nobody cheered. At the schoolyard pump a woman was distributing stored water from her own house, keeping one pot back for the morning. At the chauk, the milkman had lent his lantern to the road watch and was finding his way home by other people's windows.

The stories would come later. For now people needed directions they could follow in the dark.

---

The service lane ended at a pair of replacement boards laid across an open channel.

Fresh cable ran beneath them. Not wire one could lift with a sandal, but thick sheathed lengths held in iron saddles. Each saddle had been bolted into old stone. Removing one carelessly would let the next take its strain.

The waiting patient cart stood on the far side, too wide for the rear alley. Its driver had refused to roll a wheel over the cable. Ram thanked him before doing anything else.

Rudra looked towards the grain-store door. The watchman stood where he had left him. Inside, Rukmini's low voice continued steadily.

He let the rails down.

Vedant knelt beside the first saddle. The water in his basin drew towards it, rose against the near rim, then settled when he moved the basin back.

"Abhi charge jama hai," he said. "Khula nahi."

Gathering pressure. Not yet released.

The distinction mattered. On the river side, between the three spars, the tarpaulin had been taken down. An iron frame enclosed the service winch and a set of narrow nested rings. Beyond them the river remained river, dark and continuous under ordinary night air.

No opening stood there yet.

Rudra could see the fittings that would guide one. He did not need to know what waited on the other side to want the patient out first.

Two workmen were winding the last slack onto a drum. Another fitted a polished plate between the rings. They wore contractor's clothes without a contractor's name. When the watchman called for their order, nobody produced paper.

At the portable desk above the frame, a man in a dark sleeve closed one instrument case and opened another. His face stayed behind the raised lid.

Only his hand was visible against the dial.

Ram turned to the volunteers. "Patient ko andar wale raste se. Cart chhodo."

The driver took off his shawl to pad the wooden rails. With the watchman at one end, he went inside to help move the woman without bending her leg.

Rudra stayed at the turn where the rails would need space. He pushed a grain bin back against the wall. It scraped loudly. The sound made every worker at the frame stop for a moment.

Then their hands resumed.

That quiet was worse than a shouted threat.

---

Ira asked to be brought level with the service stair.

She did not uncover the injured eye. With the other she could see enough of the exposed plates to recognize their angles.

"Woh teen returns," she said. "Alag-alag set hain."

One plate faced the ground. Another the bank-side guide. The third caught a slice of the upper terrace in its polished hollow. Each had a different row of slots.

The resemblance to the recovered fittings was not proof of what every part did. It was enough to explain why Vedant had refused to cut blindly.

"Humara jawab inke paas hai," she told Rudra.

He looked at the exposed fourth mount. Its plate sat crooked, interrupted by a fine split across the centre.

"Mera bhi?"

"Jo humne diya. Poora tum nahi."

He took the distinction without making it larger than she had meant.

Behind them the first two helpers brought the sleeping boy through the rear doorway. He woke halfway across the turn and asked for his mother. A volunteer took him to the watch post, where he could keep seeing the door.

The patient would be next. Rukmini would walk beside her head.

Then the low vibration beneath the road changed.

Not louder. Nearer the surface.

The white cloth tied above the old evacuation turn pulled sideways although the air was still. Its knot tightened round the nail. Farther down the lane, water rose from a drain in a narrow, trembling ridge and stayed there.

The public passage had not vanished. It had acquired a boundary no one could step through without touching what held the water upright.

Vedant took one pace towards it and stopped before his basin's surface could lift.

"Udhar nahi," he told the volunteers. "Abhi kisi cheez ka jawab mat dena."

The command went down the line: no power into the waiting instruments, no axe into a loaded cable, no rush into the marked turn. People moved to the house-walls. The watchman took the boy another few steps back.

They had a little room. They did not have the route they had inspected an hour earlier.

At the desk the dark-sleeved hand laid a punched card beside the dial. Rudra was too far away to read it. He could see the pencilled mark beneath its printed line: a small lamp beside a doorway.

He looked back.

His mother had come to the grain-store threshold with a lamp. The patient lay on the padded rails behind her, ready to move. Ram was taking the upper end. Rukmini held the light low so the driver's feet would find the turn.

She saw Rudra and raised her free hand, the ordinary sign for *move a little, let us through*.

He moved.

The four-cadence struck his chest before he reached her.

Gold tightened beneath his collar. He could not get the next breath to finish on nine. After everything in the paath-shala, the two demands were still there, unchanged, making their separate claims on the same ribs.

No new shape came to meet them.

He planted his feet where his mother could see him and kept his hands open.

At the portable desk, the hand by the unpulled lever adjusted a narrow sighting pointer.

Up one notch.

From Rukmini's wrist to her throat.

---

---

# CHAPTER FIFTY-SIX — THE BREATH THAT WAS HIS

---

> *"The lamp arrived separately from the woman. In the first report they were entered on the same line.*
> *Strike that out. We carried a person. The lamp was delivered later."*
>
> *— emergency transport register, Manikarnika, correction in the receiving attendant's hand*

---

"Maa. Neeche."

Rudra did not raise his voice. There was no time to find a louder one.

Rukmini bent, putting the lamp on the threshold. Ram saw his son's face and lowered the stretcher's upper end. Behind the patient, the driver crouched with both padded rails still in his hands.

At the portable desk, the dark sleeve moved.

The lever went down.

Every iron saddle across the service lane took its load at once. The replacement boards sprang upwards. One struck Rudra across the shins and put him on his knees before he reached the door.

The sighting pointer passed over his mother's bent head.

A line opened in the wall where her throat had been.

White dust fell onto her hair. She put a hand over the patient's face before looking at the cut stone above them.

Then the rings beside the winch turned.

The river between them ceased to be visible.

Not hidden by smoke. Rudra could still see smoke crossing the gap, thin blue threads from the funeral fires farther along the ghat. Where the threads entered the iron frame, they went down instead of across. They disappeared against a strip of darkness no wider than his wrist.

The strip widened.

Cold came out of it, dry enough to carry the smell of old ash without the river's damp. A loosened bolt fell from the frame. Rudra heard it strike stone below, much farther below than the steps could possibly go.

Vedant stood up from the cable saddle.

"Darwaaza khul gaya. Peechhe ki gali khaali karo."

The command travelled away from the river. Not a call to attack. Room for the injured, first.

At the desk a punched card dropped into a receiving tray. The man behind the lid moved one finger along its edge and set a second handle at an angle.

Water rose from the drain between Rudra and the grain-store door.

He knew the first barrier had been farther down the passage. This one stood across the few feet he still had to cross. A flat wet ridge, knee-high, holding loose grit in its face. He touched a broom handle to it.

The wood came back with a clean bite taken out of it.

Ram had seen.

"Haath mat lagana."

Rudra threw the broken handle aside. "Andar jagah hai?"

"Patient ke peechhe. Bas."

The rear room had been safe because it had no river-facing window. Now pressure was entering through the service channel beneath it. The grain bins shivered against their wall. A sack seam opened, spilling wheat across the stone.

Rukmini moved the sleeping cloth away from the patient's mouth. The woman had begun to gasp.

"Meri taraf dekho," she said. "Pair seedha hai. Humne moda nahi."

The patient stared at her, trying to believe the part she could not feel through pain.

Rudra gripped the edge of a sound paving slab. He wanted to tear a path through the barrier with his bare hands. Under his collar the gold tightened at the thought; the four-cadence offered its first short push.

He released the slab.

Not because he had no strength. Because he had seen what an answer did when the instruments were waiting for it.

Behind him Veer got down from the supply cart. His left arm stayed in its sling. He planted his good foot, lowered the bad knee onto the rolled sack, and set a pale spear against the broken board nearest the cable.

"Rasta dikhata hoon," he said. "Chhota sa."

The joined bands across his shoulders lit. A narrow protective curve pressed against the wet ridge.

For a moment it made a gap.

At the frame, the plate facing the bank-side guide turned one slot. The ridge folded round the curve, closing over it from both sides.

Veer pulled his hand back before the returning edge could reach his wrist.

His right elbow was bleeding through its new dressing.

Ira heard the scrape and asked Sitara to move her towards the terrace wall, not towards the water. Her left eye remained covered. With her right she watched the exposed plates turn.

"Shield ki jagah yaad hai isko," she said.

Rudra looked at the man behind the desk lid. The same hand that had adjusted his mother's sightline rested beside the second handle. Its middle finger wore a guard of polished metal, the sort used to guide paper into a punch.

The receiving tray carried two stamped words: RIGHT HAND.

No name. No face offered in return.

Rudra got one foot beneath him. "Seedhi taraf se nahi jaa sakte?"

Dhruv was already above the first service stair, holding back the crowd with his body. Beyond him, mourners were trying to lift a bier without tipping it. The riverward steps had acquired the same standing ridges of water.

"Wahan bhi," he called.

A boy slipped beside the bier. Dhruv caught his upper arm and put him back with the men carrying it. Nobody abandoned what they had brought to the ghat.

Rudra turned back to his mother.

She had wedged a folded blanket beneath the patient's shoulders. Now she took off her own shawl and handed it to Ram to pad the rail where the bandages had begun slipping.

"Lamp bahar pahuncha do," she told Rudra, nodding at the little flame between them. "Pairon mein aa raha hai."

A household instruction. Clear the thing someone might trip over.

He found that he could obey it. Using the unbroken end of the broom, he drew the lamp round the edge of the ridge to the safe side.

The pointer at the desk did not follow it.

It remained aimed into the room.

---

Aadhya could see a way beneath the ridge. She could not yet see a way to make it safe.

The channel ran under the threshold, not through its centre. On the wall side, a peepal root from the neighbouring well had lifted a corner of the old paving. She had caught her sandal on it coming in. Now the loose stone rose and fell with each small adjustment at the frame.

The instruments were using the wet channel. The root occupied a little strip of ground beside it.

She knelt there.

Grit entered the dressing beneath her right palm. The thumb and index finger still gave her no warning of pressure; she had to look at them to make certain they were not folded underneath. She moved them straight with her left hand, then put down the heel of the palm.

"Is patthar ko haath mat lagana," she told Rudra.

He stopped reaching for it.

Green lit the skin above her bandages. The change travelled through her arms and settled across her shoulders, the fuller reach she had found at the west threshold. It did not bring back sensation to the two fingers.

She felt the root's grip under the paving. Small. Old. Not enough to lift the room.

Enough, perhaps, to stop one stone dropping while people passed it.

"Veer. Ek saans ka source de sakte ho?"

He shifted his right elbow off the bloody cart rail. "Kahan?"

She pointed to the low corner of the barrier. "Wahan nahi. Ira ki taraf."

Ira understood before Aadhya finished.

"Mujhe angle bolna padega. Main depth nahi dekh paa rahi."

Sitara put a folded sack beneath Ira's knees. She did not lift the eye covering or suggest that need had made the injury less real.

Vedant moved to the riverward side of the channel with his basin. Dhruv stayed with the people above the steps. Ram listened from inside the door, hands on the padded rails.

The whole opening was narrower than a man's shoulders. They would feed the stretcher through lengthways, keeping its long edge close to the wall. The waiting cart could remain where it was.

"Pehle maa-bete ko dekhne do," Rukmini said.

A volunteer lifted the patient's boy high enough for her to see him beyond the threshold. The woman reached towards him and nearly twisted her injured leg.

"Seedha," Ram said. Not sharply. A reminder.

She lay back.

Aadhya let the root take the paving stone's weight. A line of warm strain crossed her back. At the neighbouring well the peepal's nearest leaves began to tremble, drawing the attention of a man waiting there with an empty bucket.

"Ped ke neeche se hato," she called.

He stepped away at once.

Veer's pale curve opened over his good hand. Ira turned her uncovered eye towards it. A second curve appeared near the ground, the same width, the same thin interruption at its lower edge.

"Wall se ek ungli," Aadhya said. "Bas. Ab ghumao."

The reflection turned edge-on to the wet ridge. Rather than try to hold the whole barrier back, it divided the narrow place where the pressure returned into the channel.

Vedant tipped his basin. The little moving edge of his water caught the released trickle and led it into the empty drain on his side.

The ridge lowered by a hand's breadth.

"Ab," Aadhya said.

Ram and the driver lifted the rails. Rudra took the leading end as it emerged, keeping his hands on wood, away from the wet face of the boundary. The blanket went through. The woman's feet, braced straight beneath the shawl. Her hips.

She made a sound when the stretcher tilted.

"Seedha hai," Rukmini told her again. "Humne chhoda nahi."

Aadhya felt the root bark strip against a stone. Her right palm was bleeding into its cloth, invisible until the red reached the edge.

She kept looking at the two numb fingers. Still straight. Still clear of the opening.

At the desk a narrow plate turned towards the divided return. The frame's note changed pitch.

"Jaldi," Ira said.

Her reflected curve flickered. Veer's source had begun to close as his ribs resisted the sustained breath.

The patient's shoulders cleared the threshold.

Rudra and the driver took her weight fully. Ram let go of the upper rail only when the volunteer on the far side said he had it. They moved towards the watch post, where the boy was calling to his mother.

Aadhya released the root before it tore out of the paving.

"Band."

Veer lowered his hand. Ira's reflection went with it. Sitara caught her shoulder as she leaned too far to follow the stretcher with her good eye.

For one second the doorway was clear.

Ram turned back for Rukmini.

The opening in the rings gave a sound like a heavy breath drawn through stone. The grain-store roof shifted towards the river. A section of its inner lintel came loose where the first shot had weakened the wall below it.

Ram got his shoulder beneath the falling end.

"Rukmini. Neeche se niklo."

She bent to come under his arm.

The ridge snapped upright between them and the lane.

Aadhya reached for the root again. The green answered; the stripped bark did not. Soil loosened under her palm. She stopped before she could pull the small living hold apart.

At the portable desk, the Right Hand moved the sighting handle rather than the main lever.

Rukmini put her hand against the wall to steady herself. A narrow red line appeared across the outer edge of her shoulder.

Her fingers opened.

---

Rudra saw her hand open before he saw the blood.

For a moment the two things refused to belong together. That was the hand which had put food aside for him. Which had rested on his chest before the drill. It should have been able to close again.

Blood darkened the fabric from the outside of her left shoulder towards the collarbone. She pressed her right hand over it and backed against the inner wall.

The sighting handle moved a little farther.

Another thin cut opened above the first.

"Ruko," Rudra said.

He did not know to whom.

Ram pulled a loose packing batten from beside the grain bin and drove one end beneath the lintel. The beam settled onto it, and for half a second he could move. He reached Rukmini, pulled her away from the first line, and pressed a clean dressing from the stack by the bench against the bleeding shoulder.

The force followed.

It struck the batten where the beam rested. Wood split. Ram got his shoulder under the lintel again before the falling stone could reach her.

The bandage across his palm had opened. He wiped that hand down his shirt and gripped the broken upper length of the batten.

"Kapda dabaye raho," he told her. "Meri taraf mat mudna."

Rukmini stayed facing the lane. Her right hand was red to the wrist.

Rudra put his right foot into the wet ridge.

Pain cut across his ankle. He pulled back with blood running into his sandal, furious that the body could still retreat without consulting him.

Veer caught him by the back of his shirt.

"Seedha nahi."

"Chhod."

Veer let go. Not because he agreed. Because keeping him by force would take the only good arm with which he could still help.

He shoved the timber brace into the ridge instead. The wet edge scored it, slowed, then began eating through. For a moment the solid wood interrupted its line.

Rudra lunged through the opening.

At the frame, the fourth plate jolted on its broken mount.

The four-cadence seized his chest. His right shoulder locked behind him while the left tried to go forward. He hit the threshold on one hip, close enough now to see the grain dust stuck to his mother's blood.

Not close enough to touch her.

Dhruv came down from the upper stair after handing the crowd to the watchmen. He set his angle-ring against the sound jamb outside the room. A thin pale line crossed the falling lintel.

Ram gained an inch beneath it. Enough to shift the splintered support. Not enough to leave the beam unsupported.

"Neeche ka patthar," Dhruv told Aadhya.

She helped a volunteer push a dry wedge beneath the remaining foot of the batten. No green this time. The root beside the channel had already begun lifting out of its bed. Pulling harder would bring down the paving beneath all of them.

Ira sat against the terrace wall with Sitara's hand on her shoulder. She kept the injured eye covered. With her good one she watched the sighting bars.

"Rudra. Gardan wala handle. Doosra."

He could see it. Above the desk, the Right Hand's finger-guard flashed as the handle advanced over a short toothed scale.

No hurried movement. One tooth, then a pause while the reading settled. Another tooth.

The pressure at Rukmini's shoulder travelled upwards.

She pulled the blood-soaked cloth higher with her right hand. Her elbow shook. The cloth slipped.

Rudra tried the nine-breath allowance. Not to find a form. To get enough air to stand.

The first intake met the four halfway through.

Heat opened along the new line at the base of his neck. He tasted blood where he had bitten the inside of his mouth. The dark pressure gathered in his right hand, eager and useless while he could not turn the hand towards what he needed to stop.

He had failed this an hour ago. He could remember every instruction. Remembering did not make his lungs obey.

"Rudra," his mother said.

It was not a summons to become something. Her voice was low with pain. She was looking at the blood on his ankle.

Even then.

"Main aa raha hoon," he told her.

Ram thrust the broken length of batten across the moving line below her chin. The pressure bit into it. He held it with the bandaged hand while his shoulder kept the lintel from falling.

For a few seconds it held the edge away from her throat.

Rukmini could not lift her left arm. With the right she tried to keep pressure on the shoulder and could not manage both. Blood ran between her fingers. Her knees bent; the force at her neck kept her from sliding down the wall.

Rudra saw that it was holding her up.

The understanding emptied him.

He could not retreat to stop himself hurting someone. Could not let the failed outline break into an empty yard. There was no empty space left in front of him. His mother occupied it, and the next movement of that patient hand at the desk would cut through the wood.

The inside of the held bell closed round him for one blind instant.

He heard no answer there. No new number. No instruction he had missed during nineteen years of mornings.

Only the effort of drawing air, and the need to reach her before she could not draw any at all.

Outside, the broken wood began to split lengthways.

Ram drove his thumb into the opening to keep the two halves together.

The handle reached its last tooth.

Rukmini's mouth opened. The sound that came from it was not enough to be a breath.

---

Rudra took the air without finishing either count.

It hurt. The gold tightened as if the breath were tearing him out of an old fastening. The red-black pressure struck against it, found no agreement, and came through anyway.

This time it came through with him.

He had no other chest to give it. No uninjured body waiting beyond this one. He put his bleeding foot under his hip and rose towards his mother's open mouth.

A sound left his own, low and clear.

**"UNBOUND: JAAGRUT."**

Skin parted along the gold line above his collar. Blood ran into the shirt Rukmini had trimmed for him. The force in his hand turned his wrist until the old scar burned, then stopped turning it.

His fingers closed because he chose to close them.

Red-black gathered against his skin, dense enough that the lamp on the ground threw no light through it. Gold ran through that darkness in broken branching lines, outlining muscle, torn cloth, the flex of a hand. The colours crossed without becoming one colour. Where they met, the air gave a thin metallic shiver.

Veer took one involuntary step back.

Rudra heard it. The sound hurt somewhere the seam did not reach.

Then the wood at his mother's throat broke.

He put his forearm into the moving line.

The impact drove his new wound open. His feet slid across stone. For a moment he thought the line would take him with the wood, straighten both of them against his mother's neck and finish what the hand at the desk had begun.

He bent his elbow.

The red-black force thickened along it. Gold held the outline of the bend, bright enough for him to see the bones of his own fingers through the light. He could feel the two demands still fighting inside his ribs. Neither had learned the other's arithmetic.

His arm stayed where he put it.

The edge moved an inch away from Rukmini's throat.

Air entered her with a sound that made Ram shut his eyes for half a heartbeat.

"Neeche baitho," Rudra told her.

Her knees went. He caught the uninjured side of her back before her head could strike the wall. The shoulder cloth slipped; blood followed it, warm over the heel of his hand.

He had reached her. That had not closed the wound.

The force above his forearm tried to fold round it. A second line bit at the gold along his neck. He turned his whole body between the sighting desk and his mother, taking away the straight path instead of trying to become stronger along its length.

For one breath, his shadow covered her.

"Ab," he said.

Sitara came through the space behind his back. She had left Ira seated against the sound wall, within the watchwoman's reach. Now she put both hands beneath Rukmini's good shoulder and eased her through the doorway, keeping the injured arm from dragging.

Aadhya passed her a folded clean dressing from the aid-room parcel. She could not hold her own fingers steady; she would not trust the failing green inside an open wound. She pressed the spare cloth into Sitara's hand and made room for the work she could actually do.

Dhruv's pale line steadied the lintel while the volunteer drove the wedge the last inch under the broken prop.

"Hua," Dhruv said.

Ram took his shoulder out slowly. The stone held on wood and wedge. He came through beside his wife without testing how long the improvised support would last.

Rudra backed after them.

The pressure followed him to the threshold. He caught its narrow returning edge with his other hand, felt the red-black thicken against it, and pulled the two edges apart.

Not far. A man's width.

Enough for the last volunteer to get clear.

Then he turned the loose force towards the empty lower service steps.

Dust burst off the stone. A broken water pot went down the stair in fragments. Nobody stood there now; Dhruv had cleared that side before coming to the room.

The wet ridge across the doorway collapsed.

At the desk the sighting handle kicked against its stop. The hand above it lifted sharply. A dark fleck of blood appeared beside the dial, where the finger-guard had driven into skin.

The main lever remained down.

Between the rings the opening widened another fraction.

---

Rukmini lay on the supply cart with a rolled blanket beneath her good side.

Veer had pulled his own knee sack off it and folded the clean face uppermost. Sitara held the dressing against the shoulder while the watchwoman checked whether the bleeding had slowed. The blood was dark, steady, still much too much.

Rukmini drew another breath. Coughed. Opened her eyes.

Rudra crouched where she could see him.

The gold and darkness moved with him. Her gaze travelled across his torn collar, the changed shape of the light round his shoulders, the blood on his neck. He could not tell whether she was afraid of him or for him, and for one dreadful moment he needed the difference more than he needed air.

"Maa. Main—"

"Rudra," she said.

Hoarse. Painful. His name, with no hesitation in it.

He nodded too quickly.

Ram put a hand near the dressing, checked Sitara's grip and called for the waiting ambulance to be brought to the nearest open lane. His own palm was wet through the bandage. He wrapped it in the loose end of his shirt rather than take cloth from his wife.

Then he laid two fingers beside the dressing. His lips moved over words Rudra could not hear. Warmth passed beneath them. The steady flow into the cloth slowed, though the wound remained open. Ram's hand shook when he lifted it; his own raw palm was no better.

"Hospital. Abhi," he said.

Rudra reached for the cart handle. A little red-black pressure gathered around the wood. It began to creak.

He opened his hand at once.

"Tum nahi," Ram said.

For a moment Rudra could hear only the refusal.

Then his father put the handle in the driver's hands and moved himself between the cart and the river.

A place for each of them. Not a judgement on who was allowed to love her.

Rudra rose to stand beside him.

The form rose with him and held.

---

The cart could turn in the rear alley. Rukmini's legs did not need the long straight support the first patient had required. That small, cruel difference gave the rescuers a route.

A householder opened both doors of his inner courtyard to avoid the blocked corner. The watchwoman went first. Sitara stayed beside Rukmini's head; Aadhya helped the driver steer, pressing with her left palm. The patient's boy watched them pass from beside his mother's bench, no longer asking to go down the steps.

"Main saath hoon," Sitara told Ram.

He nodded without taking his eyes off his wife.
