Rudra kept the space between the cart and the river clear. He could feel the pressure gathering against his back. It wanted the doorway again, the reference point where the lamp and the woman had stood. He moved sideways with the cart until the line fell against him rather than the turning wheels.

His cut ankle hurt with every step. The form carried weight through it; it did not make the torn skin whole.

The first volunteer through the courtyard stopped when Rudra's shadow crossed the paving. Fear moved across the man's face before recognition did.

Rudra lowered his hands.

"Raasta khaali rakho," he said.

The man moved. Not away from him. Ahead, to hold the second door.

Behind the last turn, the ambulance's waiting light flashed against a pale wall. The cart disappeared towards it with Sitara walking at its head.

Rudra listened until he could distinguish the driver's instructions from the ringing in his ears.

Then he turned towards the frame.

---

"Akele nahi," Ram said.

"Aap bhi nahi."

His father glanced at him. Neither made it into a challenge.

Vedant stood beside the opened service channel, keeping the released water below the level of the occupied lane. Dhruv had secured the lintel and returned to the river stair. The remaining mourners were moving onto the upper landing, carrying the bier level between them.

Veer stayed by Ira. His spear was gone. He had a length of ordinary wood across his knees and was using it to point out the exposed fittings without making her turn the covered eye towards them.

"Shoulder wala toot sakta hai," Ira called to Rudra. "Main frame ko mat kheenchna. Neeche sab judaa hai."

The sighting assembly faced the room. The larger nested rings stood behind it, anchored through cables that went under the steps. They were not the same target.

Rudra nodded.

The Right Hand reached for the sighting handle again.

A new line struck the stone in front of Rudra's feet.

He moved before it climbed.

Not a leap that took him over the whole stair. One hard step onto the dry strip beside the channel, a turn of the hip, the same small change of direction Ram had corrected through a thousand mornings. The new force arrived with the movement rather than dragging his shoulder behind it.

Stone cracked beneath his heel.

He shortened the next step.

Gold tightened across his ribs. The darker pressure pressed for a straight rush at the desk. He could feel how easy it would be to mistake that urgency for his own decision.

He went towards the sighting assembly instead.

One of the workmen swung a cable hook across his path. Rudra caught the shaft below the metal head, turned it down and let the man's own momentum take him to the ground. The force in Rudra's fingers cracked the shaft after the man had released it.

He stared at the split wood for half a heartbeat.

Then he put it down. Hands empty again.

At the assembly a polished lip opened towards his chest. He brought his forearm across it as the pressure struck.

The impact took him back a step. His shoulder jarred in its socket. Red-black thickened along the blow, threaded through with gold that held the shape of his bent elbow. The form did not split down the centre.

He was still there when the first impact ended.

He got his other hand beneath the sighting bracket.

Iron bent slowly. Not an effortless tearing. The bolts dragged through their slots; one came away with a piece of stone attached. The edge of the plate cut across his left knuckles. He kept the sharp face turned away from the lane and wrenched the assembly down onto the empty step beside him.

The sighting handle at the desk fell loose.

Its pointer struck the lower stop and stayed there.

The grain-store doorway was no longer under aim.

For one second Rudra heard the ordinary river beyond the opening: a boat-chain knocking, water against a hull. He had gained something real. A line no longer following the cart. A way his father could take back towards his mother when the stair was clear.

It was not the machine's defeat.

The main lever was still down.

---

The Right Hand took the damaged sighting card out of its slot. Blood from the finger-guard had marked one corner. He pressed that corner against the cut for a moment, folded it away from the reading surface, and laid the card flat.

Then he turned the fourth mount towards Rudra.

The split in its plate remained visible. Through it a sliver of the opening showed black against black.

Rudra felt his next breath catch at the base of his neck.

The new wound was bleeding again. Gold held close along its edges without closing them. Under it the four still beat against the longer allowance, each insisting on a rhythm his body had not learned to bear for long.

He planted his feet. Took air.

The needle on the fourth scale moved.

Ira sat up against the wall.

"Rudra. Hilna mat."

He went still.

The needle moved again.

Not with his arm. Not with his feet.

With his chest.

He could see the Right Hand making one small correction at the base of the plate. No new order, no lifted main lever. Just an adjustment to a reading that had been broken since the first failed attempt.

Rudra let the breath go.

Across the scale, the fourth trace completed its return.

He drew another.

Between the iron rings, the lower edge of the opening moved with it.

---

---

# CHAPTER FIFTY-SEVEN — THE LINE THEY COULD CUT

---

> *"One return line isolated. Upper passage released. Main assembly still live.*
> *Do not file the first two entries under failed demolition."*
>
> *— riverwatch cable-recovery sheet, first entry, countersigned after the fighting*

---

Rudra held the breath.

The fourth needle stopped. Beneath it, a toothed wheel clicked twice more. The opening between the rings continued widening.

"Saans lo," Ram said.

Rudra shook his head.

"Dekho pehle. Saans ruki. Darwaaza nahi."

His father pointed with the bandaged hand. The needle was still, but the wheel below it had caught on another tooth. At the desk the Right Hand moved a short handle, and the lower ring advanced without waiting for Rudra's chest.

Rudra let the air out.

It scraped along the raw line above his collar. Red-black pressure tightened over his ribs, crossed by gold which held the outline together without making it painless. The next intake came too quickly. He had to put a hand against the broken sighting bracket to steady himself.

The bracket bent under his palm.

He took the hand away.

"Main isko chala raha hoon?" he asked.

"Abhi itna pata hai ki tumko padh raha hai," Ram said. "Baaki hum dekhenge. Tum saans lo."

Ira had risen beside the terrace wall. Veer kept his good hand beneath her elbow until she found the edge with the back of her own hand. Her left eye remained covered. The cloth had dried stiff where it touched her cheek.

"Needle aur neeche ka wheel alag hain," she said. "Main dono ek saath nahi dekh paa rahi."

"Wheel main dekhunga," Veer answered.

He tapped the wood across his knees each time it moved. Ira watched the needle.

Two sounds, a pause, one sound. Then another pause in which the needle moved and Veer's hand did not.

Rudra could hear the difference from the stair.

The sighting plate had followed his chest. The winch could still turn without it. Holding his breath was not a way out.

He looked towards the courtyard through which his mother had gone.

A man came back from its inner door carrying the empty board that had bridged the courtyard drain. He held it upright to keep its wet end off his clothes. Behind him, the ambulance's light moved across the upper wall and disappeared.

"Gaadi nikal gayi," he called. "Bahan saath mein hain."

Ram turned. "Saans?"

The man understood. "Chal rahi thi. Darwaaza band kiya tab boli bhi."

He could tell them no more. Sitara had gone with her. The road beyond the courtyard remained open to the ambulance.

Ram nodded once. The muscles beside his jaw loosened and tightened again.

Rudra wanted a whole report. Blood pressure, words spoken, how the attendant had looked when the doors shut. He had one man's honest account of a few seconds. He made himself accept it without asking the man to turn it into certainty.

Aadhya came back through the courtyard after him. She had let the ambulance crew take the cart and had stayed to keep the turn clear until it left. A strip of fresh cloth covered the heel of her left palm. The right thumb and index finger were still held straight against the side of her hand, useless for gripping.

"Maa ke saath Sitara hain," she told Rudra.

"Suna."

"Pata hai. Phir bhi."

She took the wet board from the householder with her forearms rather than her fingers and laid it beside the opened channel.

They would need somewhere to kneel that did not move.

---

At the frame, the fourth plate turned a little farther towards Rudra. Its cracked face caught the gold along his neck. Beneath it a fine covered thread entered the back of the scale housing and ran down beside a cable thick as his wrist.

The narrow sleeve could have passed for a strand coming loose from the larger cable. It had its own fastening.

"Woh patla wala," he said.

Vedant looked up from the channel. "Dikha. Chhoona mat."

Rudra pointed without reaching into the gap.

The thick cable took strain from the winch to an iron saddle beneath the stair. The fine thread did not pull taut with it. Even when the larger cable shuddered, the little sleeve stayed loose.

Vedant stepped onto the dry strip and examined the place where the two entered the housing.

"Ek bojh utha raha hai," he said. "Doosra hukm le jaa raha hai. Kaunsa hukm, pehle woh dekhenge."

Across the frame the Right Hand lifted a card from its tray and changed its position under the punch. His injured finger left a dark smear on the edge. The fourth needle steadied towards Rudra again.

Water stood higher along the occupied lane.

Rudra started towards the plate.

A foot of paving lifted before his good leg came down. He changed direction, caught the edge of the sound step, and felt his cut right ankle take the turn badly. Pain struck through the form. Gold flared along his throat.

The water followed the change before he could choose the next step.

He stopped with one hand above it, not touching.

At the terrace Veer tapped once. The wheel had moved.

"Tumhare badalne se pehle jagah badal raha hai," Ira said. "Isi liye seedhe nahi pahunch paaoge."

Rudra could have driven through. He could feel enough force in his shoulder to try it. He could also see the householder still standing at the courtyard door, with people moving behind him.

Vedant pointed along the thick cable. Its saddle was bolted through the foot of the house wall. With the next click of the wheel, plaster shook loose above the bolt.

"Seedha kheenchoge toh udhar aayega," he said.

The Right Hand laid his uninjured palm on the main lever. No movement yet. Just a hand resting where everyone could see it.

Rudra lowered his shoulder.

They needed a cut that did not take the street with it.

---

Ira asked for mud before she asked anyone for power.

Aadhya scraped wet silt from the channel with the end of the courtyard board. Ram took a palmful and pressed it onto a flat scrap from the broken sighting cover. He slid the scrap towards the fourth plate along the sound edge of the plinth.

The needle slowed.

At the desk a handle moved. The plate tilted, shedding the mud off its face. The needle found Rudra again.

Veer tapped the wood once. The wheel below had moved too.

"Poora dhakna padega," Ira said.

With Veer naming the edge, Ira lowered herself onto the terrace step. Aadhya pushed the board's wet end beside her knee.

Ram withdrew the scrap before the next adjustment could trap his hand. Mud covered his bandage. The little thread behind the scale remained clean.

Covering the face would not hold. In the courtyard, the whole fitting had sat inside a clay bowl packed with wet sand. Here the operator could shake a loose covering away.

Ira could see the front. She could not see round the casing, and turning her head only shifted the missing portion of her vision to another place. The left-eye dressing did not become transparent because she wanted it to.

"Peechhe paani hai?" she asked.

Rudra moved his head without moving his feet. "Channel. Casing ke neeche tak."

"Kitna neeche?"

He held thumb and finger apart. "Itna."

She asked him to describe the gap twice. Not because she doubted him. Because the first description had made her imagine it deeper than it was.

Vedant was listening from the opened drain.

"Upar aake bhaag raha hai," he said. "Rokoge toh peechhe ki jheel banegi. Chhoti."

A small pool, using water already rising under pressure. Enough to carry silt into the lower slots and hold it there while Ram packed the face.

"Mujhe ek shape chahiye," Ira said. "Tumhare paani ki."

Veer glanced at her. "Mera wala nahi?"

"Usko pehchaanta hai. Aur tumhara haath—"

"Theek. Reason pehle wala hi rakho."

She looked at the sling, then at his freshly wrapped elbow. He knew what she was doing. For a moment his mouth almost smiled.

He settled the wooden length across his good thigh. She waited until his right elbow rested on the folded cloth again before turning towards Vedant.

---

Vedant brought his basin close without abandoning the drain. One hand remained against the old outlet stone; the water beyond it ran low instead of climbing into the lane.

With the other he held a shallow curve at the basin's rim. The water rose against that curve and stayed, a transparent lip a palm wide and no higher than Ira's little finger.

She did not reach for the water itself.

The shape holding it touched her sight. Softer than Veer's shield. Its edge changed a little even while it held still, accommodating the movement through it rather than resisting every grain.

She received that one edge.

The narrow silver lines returned to her uncovered iris. The pain behind the dressing answered at once.

A second holding curve appeared over a puddle on the board beside her knee. The puddle rose, thinned, and spilled over the side.

Too much angle.

Vedant lowered his hand to steady the basin. The reflected edge fell with his, before Ira chose to release it. Water spread into the sand beneath the board.

"Mat kheenchna," Vedant said. "Jo aa raha hai, usko thehraana."

Hold what arrived. Do not drag more towards it.

She tried again, receiving the same small pattern. This time the puddle's remaining edge stood against a pale seam in the air.

It stayed until she closed her sight.

A strike rang against stone farther down the stair. Ira looked up with ordinary vision.

Another workman had driven a long hook towards Rudra's ankle. Rudra caught its iron neck against the broken bracket, forcing the point down instead of letting the whole shaft swing towards the lane. His neck was bleeding into the collar again.

Ram moved to his other side. Between them they kept the man from reaching the rear passage.

The tests had cost seconds. Other people were paying for those seconds with their bodies.

"Ab casing," Ira said.

Vedant kept the little curve above his basin. Ira received it again and turned the new reflection towards the gap Rudra had described.

He watched it arrive. "Neeche. Ab meri taraf. Bas."

The water behind the scale began to gather. Not new water: the same grey trickle from the channel, stopped before it could run away. Silt clouded the little pool.

Ram slid the muddy scrap along the front again.

At the desk the Right Hand moved to tilt the plate.

It could turn its face. It could not empty the water held against its back.

Grit packed the lower slots. Ram pressed another fold of silt into the front seam with the flat metal, keeping his fingers out of the mounting jaws.

Ira could feel the whole shape only while Vedant maintained it. Each tiny alteration at his basin ran through the reflection. She could not make it steadier than he could supply.

"Ek aur saans," he said.

She gave it.

The fourth needle shivered, travelled halfway towards Rudra, and stopped.

The toothed wheel below clicked again.

"Needle ruki," Ira said.

"Wheel nahi," Veer answered.

That was the answer. Small, exact, worth the pain beneath her covered eye.

She released the reflected edge. Vedant lowered his own hand, and the lip of water fell back into his basin. The little pool behind the casing drained, leaving packed wet silt in its slots.

The needle stayed still.

Rudra's next breath did not move it.

A tremor passed through Ira's drawing hand so hard that her knuckles struck the board. She kept that hand on the wood until it stopped knocking.

"Main theek—" she began.

"Mat bolo," Veer said. "Baitho."

She was already sitting. He meant stay there, and let someone else look next.

She did.

---

Rudra put his good foot on the next dry stone.

This time the paving did not rise to meet it.

The water turned after him. Late enough that he saw the edge coming and could draw his injured foot clear instead of wrenching the ankle sideways.

At the desk, the Right Hand took his palm off the main lever and worked the smaller handle twice. Mud shook from the fourth casing.

"Zyada waqt nahi," Ira called.

The hook trapped against the broken bracket scraped upwards. The workman was trying to free it while Rudra watched the plate.

Ram caught the shaft. His bandage slid against the wood.

Rudra turned back. He put his forearm over the shaft and bore down, meaning only to keep it still. The man's grip opened with a cry.

Rudra lifted the pressure immediately.

The hook clattered onto stone. The man pulled his arm against his chest, fingers moving, and backed towards the stair. A pair of short cable-shears swung from his belt.

"Woh chhod," Ram said, pointing.

The man stared at him.

"Auzaar. Yahin. Phir upar."

He unclipped the shears with his unhurt hand and put them down. Ram kicked the hook beyond his reach. At the first turning Dhruv held out an arm, stopping the man from running into the mourners. A watchman took hold of his collar.

Rudra watched until the man was behind the upper wall.

He had not meant to hurt that arm. He could not call the cry an accident and stop thinking about it.

"Rudra," Ram said.

He turned back to the people still in reach.

---

The fine sleeve ran through a little iron clip before entering the scale housing. Beside it the thick cable shuddered with every tooth of the winch.

Ram set the shears down with the jaws open.

"Dono ko alag karna hai. Pehle."

He picked up a piece of the split hook-shaft Rudra had dropped beside the sighting assembly. Its blunt end fitted between the two sleeves. Pushed a little farther, it lifted the narrow one clear of the loaded cable.

Rudra reached to hold it.

The wood cracked under his fingers.

He stopped.

For a moment he wanted to throw the useless piece away and take the whole housing off the wall. Iron had bent for him. The small thing was asking more patience than the iron.

Ram moved the wood under the flat of his son's forearm.

"Pakadna nahi. Yahan rakho."

Rudra rested his arm where he was shown. No closed fist. No pull towards his chest. The wood stayed in one piece.

A bead of water ran out of the casing, carrying grey grit. Then another. The small handle at the desk moved again.

Ira's good eye followed the needle.

"Abhi ruki hai," she said. "Jaldi nahi. Saaf kaato."

Ram worked one blade beneath the isolated sleeve. The other rested above it. Thick cable on the far side of the wooden spacer. Empty air inside the jaws except for the thin line they had chosen.

His hand shook.

He took it off the handle, wiped the blood from his palm against his trousers and set both hands on the shears.

Behind Rudra the water lifted.

Veer came off the folded sack onto his good foot. A pale curve rose between the wet edge and Ram's back, no wider than it had been in the doorway.

The water struck its weak lower corner.

Veer angled it rather than asking the damaged edge to become whole. The blow slid down onto the empty stone by his knee. He grunted and kept the curve there.

"Ab wheel tum dekho," he told Ira.

He could not watch the wheel and keep the curve in place. She shifted her gaze down from the stalled needle.

"Chal raha hai," she said. "Ek daant aur."

Rudra felt that next movement in the thick cable beneath his arm. The wooden spacer dragged against his skin.

He did not press harder.

Ram closed the shears.

The covering split. The bright strand inside bent without parting.

The needle jerked once.

A thin wet line raced up the step. Veer's curve intercepted it, leaving a dark score in the stone beyond his heel.

Rudra's neck seam widened as he turned his shoulder into the remaining force. He could feel warm blood under the gold. For one breath the red-black thinned over his wrist, then gathered again without taking the wrist away from where he had put it.

Ram shifted the strand deeper between the blades.

The second squeeze cut it.

One end dropped against the wooden spacer. The other recoiled no farther than the width of Ram's thumb.

The thick cable stayed taut.

Above it, no bolt moved in the house wall.

"Hua," Ram said.

Rudra took his arm away slowly. The spacer fell free. He left both cut ends where he could see them.

Veer lowered his hand and sat down hard on the sack. His right elbow left a red patch on his shirt.

At the desk, the small handle went all the way over.

Nothing followed Rudra's next step.

Mud dripped from the fourth plate. Its needle began to move again, a small trembling answer to his chest.

Farther along the lane, the standing water kept its old angle.

The reading still existed at the plate. On this line, it could go no farther than the cut.

Rudra took one more breath, watching both the cut ends.

"Saans rokni nahi thi," he said.

Ram picked up the shears. "Rasta kaatna tha."

Below them the toothed wheel clicked again.

---

The upper passage was still blocked.

Water stood across it in three short ridges. Cutting the return line had not emptied the channel or taken away the angles already set into the plates. A loose rag touched the nearest ridge and came apart.

Vedant left it there as a warning.

"Ab yeh tumhare peechhe nahi ghoom raha," he told Rudra. "Isko neeche mod sakte hain. Wahan."

He pointed towards the cleared lower service steps, not the river stair where Dhruv's people were still moving.

Aadhya looked at the peepal root beside the grain-store threshold. The stripped portion lay pale against the broken paving. She did not put her palm over it again.

Instead she dragged the courtyard board to the channel on her forearms. She set one end on sound stone, then asked the householder to move the other until it rested beyond the undermined edge. It restored the missing foothold at the wall; the middle of the lane still had sound paving.

"Iske neeche paani jaayega," she said. "Upar ek-ek aadmi. Abhi koi nahi."

Rudra stepped into the narrow dry strip beside it. The form tightened over his shins. His right ankle had begun to swell against the sandal strap.

He brought his forearm against the wet ridge.

The first touch bit through the outline over his skin. He drew back a fraction, found the angle at which the pressure ran past rather than straight into him, and turned his shoulder with it.

Not farther than he needed.

Water struck the first empty step below them. It cut a white line through the grit.

Vedant lowered his basin. The diverted stream caught its moving edge and went down the service channel instead of circling back towards Rudra's legs.

The ridge tried to close from its old lower angle.

Rudra could see where it was coming from now. It had not followed the last movement of his shoulder. He kept the small separation open while Vedant led the released water through it.

The second ridge sank.

At the desk the Right Hand abandoned the small handle. He moved a longer one beside the main lever.

"Baayein," Ira called. "Dekha usne."

Rudra shifted before the new setting reached him. A flat blow struck the stone where his injured foot had been.

It was still a fight. The man at the desk still had eyes and hands. But now he had to see the movement and make his answer after it.

That was enough room for Vedant to finish turning the flow.

The last ridge lowered to an ordinary runnel.

Rudra kept his arm in place until the water stopped striking it. When he lifted it away, the outline shook from wrist to shoulder. The gold drew close along his neck. He tasted the old mouth wound again.

He stood without taking another step until his foot belonged under him.

"Ab," Vedant said.

Aadhya let the householder cross the board first, empty-handed. She watched both ends while he put his full weight over the middle. No rocking. No fresh crack in the stone beneath it.

Then she waved the people inside his door towards them.

An old woman came with a steel container held against her stomach. Behind her, a girl tugged at the key on her neck and turned back towards an inner cupboard. Aadhya put a forearm behind the girl's shoulders and kept her moving without closing her numb fingers round the cord.

"Chaabi rehne do," she said. "Tum chalo."

Ram stood at the far end to receive them.

He looked at the key, then at the house above the loaded saddle. He did not tell the girl she would certainly be able to use it tomorrow.

---

Dhruv brought the remaining bier-bearers across in their own formation. Nobody asked them to tip what they carried to make the turn quicker. The dry width was there now; they used it. The watchmen brought both disarmed workmen up behind the mourners, the second still cradling his arm.

The householder went back once with a watchman, checked the rear room, and came out without anyone behind him. He shut neither courtyard door.

At the watch post the first patient's boy saw people moving towards the upper lane and stood up beside his mother's bench.

"Hum bhi?"

Aadhya asked the men at the road end whether the whole straight passage was clear. She waited for an answer from the far turning, not just a wave from the nearest helper.

"Khula hai," the call came back.

Only then did she send for the padded rails.

They came with the patient still on them. Her injured leg lay straight beneath Rukmini's shawl. Two volunteers took the ends; a third walked beside the injured side without trying to lift or bend it. Her boy stayed near her head, where she could see him.

No cart turn this time. No promise that the pain would stop before the road.

Ram watched the trailing rail clear the board.

"Udhar transport waalon ko bulao," he told the watchman. "Isko yahin mat bitha dena."

The watchman went ahead to the road party. The patient left the ghat passage carried level.

Rudra followed her progress with his eyes until the shawl was out of sight.

They had not closed the opening. They had taken people away from it.

At the frame the fourth plate twitched towards his chest once more. He looked down at the severed strand lying beside its larger neighbour.

He did not need to hold his breath to keep the passage open.

"Wapas mat aana," he told the householder. "Darwaaza khula rehne do."

The man looked at his house. Then he looked at the people who had just crossed.

He gave Ram a torn strip from his own shoulder towel for the bleeding palm and went up the lane.

---

Ram wound the towel strip over his opened bandage. He pulled the knot tight with his teeth, then picked up the shears again.

Rudra looked towards the turning where the workman had gone.

"Uska haath—"

"Doctor dekhega."

"Maine bas roka tha."

Ram looked at the split hook-shaft beneath the casing.

Rudra followed his gaze. The wood had been in his hand too.

"Pata hai," Ram said.

Not permission to forget it. Not a reason to stop helping the people he could still reach. His father placed the shears on the dry step, away from Rudra's feet, and waited until Rudra looked back at the frame.

The main wheel had not missed a tooth during the evacuation.

At the desk, the Right Hand no longer touched the fourth card. It lay crooked under the punch. Both his hands were now at the foot of the main lever, one holding a small metal catch while the other worked something loose beneath it.

The finger-guard had come off. Blood marked the bare knuckle.

A long steel pin emerged from the lever's stop.

"Woh nikaal raha hai," Ira said.

She leaned forward and lost the edge of the terrace in her reduced vision. Veer caught her sleeve without rising. She put her own hand down, found stone, and stayed behind it.

"Pin. Lever ke neeche."

Ram saw it.

"Rudra. Seedha uske paas nahi. Beech ka jod dekho."

Between the nested rings, below the opening's lower edge, an iron coupling turned on a shorter shaft. The outer feet stayed fixed to the paving. The coupling carried the movement from the winch into the turning frame.

Rudra could see only part of it each time the inner band went past.

"Bahar wala kheenchoge toh cable aayega," Ram said. "Pehle isko rokne ki jagah dhoondho."

Not an order to put a hand between moving teeth. A warning against the large easy target.

Rudra moved along the dry strip, keeping the coupling in view.

---

Dhruv came down two steps from the river stair.

"Upar ki service gali khaali," he called. "Aage log hain. Udhar mat modna."

The space they had emptied was not the whole ghat. Beyond the upper turning, people still stood under roofs joined to these walls. The road party was still carrying the injured away.

Aadhya stayed at the passage mouth. She put her back against the house wall and used her shoulder to keep a loose door from swinging across the route. Her hands hung clear.

Vedant watched the channel. The water had begun pulsing against his diversion, each pulse stronger than the last.

"Rudra. Neeche ka rasta abhi khaali hai. Zyada der nahi."

Rudra put his left foot beside the coupling's stone base.

The four struck inside his chest.

The longer allowance answered too late. For an instant his right hand wanted to open while his shoulder drove forward. The gold across his throat thinned to separate bright stitches.

He stopped advancing.

Blood spotted the step below his collar. He touched the seam with his sleeve, looked at the red and kept still until he could choose a step instead of being driven into one.

Ram came level with him, outside the moving band's reach.

"Dikh raha?"

"Beech mein. Har baar aadha."

"Toh aadha dekh ke haath nahi."

They watched a full turn. Then another.

The coupling had a solid shoulder on its stair-facing side. The teeth passed behind it. Rudra lowered his forearm towards that shoulder without touching the moving shaft.

At the desk the steel pin came free.

It struck the lid and rolled against the punch.

The main lever dropped the last few inches into a deeper notch.

---

The winch gave one hard pull.

The courtyard board jumped on the empty passage. Water burst from beneath it and went down the service steps in a sheet. Vedant dropped to one knee, bringing his basin's edge low enough to keep the first surge away from Aadhya's legs.

A bolt under the frame tore out with a square of stone attached.

The outer upright pitched towards the house cable.

Rudra left the coupling and caught the falling iron with both forearms.

The load drove his left knee into the step. His right ankle folded against the stone; pain went white behind his eyes. He could not find either count in it.

The outline held.

Barely. Red-black compressed along his arms. Gold flared across the raw neck seam, and blood fell between his feet while the upright stopped moving.

At the desk the Right Hand took both hands away from the lever.

It stayed down in the notch.

The thick cable began pulling through its saddle again. From the foot of the emptied house came a crack which ran upwards, past the courtyard door they had left open.

Rudra pressed his back against the sound stone of the stair cheek.

He had caught the frame before it fell. He had not stopped the wheel inside it.

The next tooth went over while his arms were still on the iron.

---

---

# CHAPTER FIFTY-EIGHT — THE WEIGHT HE SET DOWN

---

> *"The river could be seen through the broken frame.*
> *That is the time entered for closure. It is not the time we stopped carrying the wounded."*
>
> *— Manikarnika recovery report, copy attached to the casualty list*

---

The crack reached the door hinge.

Aadhya took her shoulder off the wood. The upper fastening came out of the wall where her head had been. She backed onto the sound middle of the passage as the door fell across the empty board.

Dust covered her feet.

"Main nikal gayi," she called before Rudra could ask.

He could not turn to see. The upright lay across his forearms. Every tooth of the wheel dragged it a little farther against him; every movement travelled up the loaded cable to the house.

His left knee was on stone. His right foot would no longer lie flat.

"Ghar ke neeche se hato," Ram called towards the upper turning.

The warning went ahead of the falling plaster. People beyond the bend moved away from the adjoining wall. Dhruv came down the last two river steps with his angle-ring already in his hand.

He did not put himself beneath the iron beside Rudra.

He knelt at its loose foot and looked for somewhere to return its weight.

"Veer. Neeche ka kona. Bas kona."

Veer pushed himself up from the sack. Ira kept her hand on the terrace stone instead of taking the arm he could not offer.

The joined pale bands lit across his shoulders. His spear formed in his right hand, bright along the length, the point wavering once as he tried to lower it.

He put the butt against a sound step. The shaft came up beneath the fallen frame's lower corner.

Rudra felt a little weight leave his left arm.

Not enough to stand. Enough to stop that arm being ground into the stone.

Veer bent over the shaft. His ribs would not let him take the next full breath.

"Patthar," he said.

Dhruv caught a loose block beside the channel and began rolling it towards the lifted paving.

"Wahan nahi," Aadhya said. "Uske neeche khokhla hai."

The peepal root had lifted that paving before the fighting. Now the stripped length lay exposed beneath it. She had felt the loose bed when she tried to hold it earlier; covering it with a block would not make it a foundation.

She pointed towards the stair cheek at Rudra's back.

"Yahan. Seedhi ka hissa."

Dhruv changed his position. His heel struck the stair-side stone twice. It did not rock. He rolled the loose block onto that sound ledge, keeping his hands clear of the falling foot.

A thin pale line ran from his angle-ring to the iron's lower edge.

"Do ungli," he told Rudra. "Uthao. Kheenchna nahi."

Rudra pressed his back harder into the stair cheek. The red-black thickened along his forearms. Gold crossed the torn cloth at his neck, and fresh blood entered the collar beneath it.

The upright rose an inch.

Veer's spear shook.

Dhruv rolled the block into the gap with the edge of his ring. Ram pushed a smaller stone behind it with his shoe, preventing it from sliding back towards the channel.

"Neeche. Dheere."

Rudra let the weight return.

Stone grated. The block held between the sound ledge and the bent foot. Dhruv kept his line there until the iron had stopped moving against it.

Then Veer took the spear out.

It disappeared before he could use it as a walking stick. He lowered himself onto the terrace step with his good hand against the wall. Ira named the edge for him this time.

Rudra's left arm came free.

It would not straighten at once. He held it against his ribs until the fingers stopped trying to close without him.

"Chhodo ab," Ram said, looking at the other forearm.

Rudra tried.

The iron settled another fraction. He put the arm back.

Ram watched the block rather than his son's face.

"Aur nahi baith raha. Dekho."

Rudra looked. The block had found its bed. The next click of the wheel travelled through it into the stair, not down through his arm.

He drew the arm away.

For a moment he remained kneeling beneath something he was no longer holding.

Then Ram gave him his shoulder, and Rudra got his left foot under him. He did not put all his weight onto the cut right ankle.

---

The opening had widened while they worked.

A strip of the far bank should have been visible above the lower ring. There was only darkness there, with smoke descending through it in long straight threads. Cold slid over Rudra's bleeding wrist.

He turned his head towards the exposed coupling.

Its solid shoulder passed the stair every turn. Behind it, teeth met and parted. From the winch came the same short pull, then the click which kept the load from returning.

Ram pointed to the place where the shorter shaft entered the turning ring.

"Yeh tootega toh ghoomna rukega. Bahar ki tang kheenchoge toh ghar aayega."

Rudra could see the difference now. The part he had caught was holding the assembly upright. The smaller joint was driving its turn.

He wiped his left knuckles against his shirt. The plate cuts stung. He needed that hand to open properly, not simply become strong enough to close.

At the desk the Right Hand reached for the longer control handle.

Water rose behind Ram's heels.

Rudra pulled his father onto the dry strip before the ridge struck.

The blow split the small stone Ram had used behind the block. Dhruv's line caught the block before it shifted off the ledge.

The main lever stayed locked down.

Rudra turned towards the hand making the corrections.

They had to break more than a piece of iron.

---

"Main handle rok sakti hoon," Ira said. "Thodi der."

Veer looked from her to the desk. "Main rokun?"

"Tum yahin rehkar."

He understood the part she had not said. His knee would not carry him across that stair again. Hers was not a request to pretend otherwise.

He rested his right elbow against the folded sack. A narrow pale curve opened above his hand, its lower interruption still there.

Ira kept her covered eye towards the wall. With her right she received the live curve. The second edge appeared beside the desk, too high at first, whitening the corner of the raised lid.

"Neeche," Rudra told her.

It moved.

"Ab uski taraf. Bas."

The Right Hand tried to pull the long handle back.

Its grip met the reflected curve.

Metal rang softly. The handle stayed short of its next notch.

The main lever below it remained locked down. The wheel continued turning. Ira had stopped one correction, not the opening itself.

Rudra moved towards the coupling.

Ram caught the loose cloth at his back.

Not pulling him away. Just enough to make him look.

"Khula haath," Ram said.

Rudra looked down. His fingers were already closing.

He opened them. Gold shivered across the cut knuckles. The left hand wanted to close again; he kept the heel of it ready instead.

Ram let go of the shirt.

---

The coupling's solid shoulder came towards the stair.

Rudra put the flat of his right palm against its outer face, well outside the teeth. He meant to turn the short shaft across its own line of movement, not hold the rotating iron in his fingers.

The first pressure skated his hand sideways.

He took it off before it could be carried round.

Pain ran up the old wrist into the shoulder. He had changed. The wrist had not become another wrist.

"Usko pakadke mat rukna," Ram said. "Side se. Saath mein pair."

Rudra set his left foot closer. The bruised knee bent badly. He lowered his hip to keep the weight off the cut ankle and waited for the solid face to come round again.

Behind him Veer breathed out through his teeth.

Ira's reflected edge wavered at precisely the same moment as his source. She did not fill its weak corner by wanting it filled. She brought the whole curve a little nearer the handle instead.

At the desk the bare injured hand withdrew.

The other reached across the lid towards the steel stop-pin.

"Pin le raha hai," she called.

Rudra could not look away from the teeth now.

The solid shoulder arrived.

He met it with the heel of his palm and moved his body across it, a short drive from the left hip. Red-black gathered against the iron. Gold held the line from wrist to elbow while the two counts fought for the breath beneath his ribs.

His right ankle dragged. The cut reopened against the sandal strap.

He kept moving sideways, away from the teeth, forcing the shorter shaft out of its straight alignment.

A bolt bent.

The coupling screamed against its collar.

The next click did not come.

The opening stopped widening.

Rudra felt the load seeking the house cable again and eased his shoulder rather than pressing the whole frame towards it. Ram pointed to the bent joint.

"Udhar. Jo mud gaya. Wahi."

A crack had opened across the coupling's collar. The joint was no longer turning cleanly; its bent face had jammed against the fixed housing beside it.

Rudra took his hand away.

The joint stayed jammed.

The house cable remained taut, but no new length travelled through its saddle. Beside the courtyard door the crack in the plaster stopped dropping dust.

"Ruki?" Aadhya called.

"Badhna ruka," Ram answered. "Band nahi hua."

Through the rings the darkness remained. Smoke still descended where the river should have been.

One result. Not a finished fight.

---

Ira's uncovered eye had begun to water. She could see the handle, but its edges were spreading into the glare of her own reflection.

"Veer. Chhodne ko bolna."

"Bolunga."

"Pehle bolna."

His right hand trembled. She could see it. That was why she needed the words before the light disappeared, not a brave nod after it.

"Chhod raha hoon," he said.

She released the reflection. His curve fell a moment later. Nothing stayed beside the desk.

Ira leaned back against the terrace stone. Fresh moisture warmed the edge of her left-eye dressing. She did not touch it with the hand which had been against grit and water.

At the desk the steel pin passed through the space the reflected curve had occupied. The long handle came free behind it.

"Ram," Ira said.

He was already looking.

The Right Hand drove the control over, towards the place where Ram stood between the joint and his son.

Rudra saw the water flatten along the channel.

Ram shoved him off its first line.

The blow took Ram across the right side and threw him against the fixed iron base.

The impact made a short, dull sound.

Rudra heard it through the strain of the iron.

---

Ram tried to get a hand beneath himself.

It slipped. Not on water. The towel round his palm had opened again.

Rudra reached him as he tried a second time.

"Mat utho."

His father looked at the jammed coupling over his shoulder. He drew a short breath, stopped halfway through it, and pressed his other hand against his lower right ribs.

Aadhya knelt on the dry side of the base. She set her forearm behind his shoulders without pulling him upright.

"Saans?" she asked.

Ram nodded.

The next one was shorter.

"Upar se medical waalon ko bulao," she called. "Abhi."

Veer repeated it towards the road. A watchman's answer came back from the turning. Feet started down the sound middle of the passage, away from the fallen door.

Aadhya kept her numb fingers out from under Ram's back. She could feel his next breath against her forearm. Uneven. Present.

Rudra wanted to stay with that one fact until it became enough.

At the desk the long handle moved again.

The water beside his father's knee drew into a flat shining edge.

Rudra stood between it and the two people on the ground.

He took the blow across his forearm. The impact drove his elbow against his chest and opened the seam above his collar. Gold broke into bright separate stitches. The darker outline held at the arm, thin at the shoulder, and he could not make the two counts agree enough to stop the shaking.

He turned with the force instead.

Water struck the empty lower step. Vedant caught its freed edge with the flow from his basin and sent it down the service channel.

Rudra looked towards the desk.

"Ab meri taraf dekh."

---

He did not jump the space.

The cut ankle would not give him that. He took the two short steps beside the winch, using the sound paving he had watched Ram cross, and put his left forearm across the long handle before it could return to its next setting.

The Right Hand let go of it.

The steel pin came up beneath Rudra's arm.

He saw its point too late to catch it. He turned his shoulder instead. Iron scraped along the outside of his upper left arm, opening cloth and skin instead of entering beneath his ribs.

No wall of power had made the flesh untouchable.

Rudra pushed the weapon arm out with his own elbow. His fingers wanted to close round the wrist. He did not trust what would happen if they did.

The man stepped inside that push.

A boot struck Rudra's swollen right ankle.

His leg folded.

The pin turned towards the raw line at his throat.

He let himself go down another inch instead of wrenching the damaged foot round. The point passed over his shoulder. He came inside the striking elbow, close enough that the long pin had no room to turn back towards him.

His left knee hit the plinth.

Pain emptied his mouth of the breath he had been trying to hold. The outline flickered along his jaw.

The Right Hand drove his free palm against that same shoulder.

Rudra slid. Caught the plinth's edge with his left foot. Felt the bruised knee threaten to give again.

He could have answered with everything in his right hand.

The force was there, hot and dense. He could feel it asking for the shortest path through the body in front of him. He could also remember his father's bloody palm slipping against the base, and the workman cradling an arm Rudra had only meant to stop.

He put his forearm over the elbow of the weapon arm instead.

Wood took the first pressure. The desk's edge split beneath the man's arm.

Rudra kept his palm open and drove the trapped forearm down onto the wood, away from the throat, away from the controls.

The pin fell.

He moved his arm off the elbow at once.

The Right Hand reached for the handle with his injured hand.

Rudra got his other forearm beneath the handle and drove it sideways. Its thin mount tore. The handle struck the raised lid and folded it back. One desk-leg went under as the mount tore through the wooden top.

The man lost his footing as the desk went down.

His back struck the low service ledge behind it. One knee folded beneath the fallen wood. He tried to rise on the injured hand, failed, and caught himself on the other elbow.

The control handle lay between him and Rudra, its narrow linkage torn out with the mount.

Rudra planted a foot on the steel pin before that empty hand could reach it.

He did not plant it on the hand.

For a moment they remained there, breathing on opposite sides of the broken lid.

Then the Right Hand drew his arm back against his body.

He was down. Disarmed. No longer making the pressure turn.

Behind Rudra, Aadhya called for the medical men again.

---

The main lever was still locked in its deeper notch.

Rudra could hear the drive straining against the joint he had jammed. Leaving the man on the stone was not enough.

He took his foot off the pin and turned to the lever's stop-plate.

He worked his forearm beneath the little iron throat that held the lever down. The first push bent its corner. He shifted closer, kept the force in that small plate, and tore it off its mount.

The main lever sprang up.

The deep note beneath the winch stopped.

The cable did not become loose. A toothed catch still held the wheel against the weight already on it.

But nothing was pulling it farther into the house.

Rudra let the broken stop-plate fall onto the empty step.

"Band karte hain," he told Dhruv.

---

A road medic came down with two watchmen and a folded canvas stretcher.

He stopped at the bend, saw the fallen door, and followed Aadhya's voice along the sound middle of the lane. The frame stood between him and the man behind the desk. He went to the patient he could reach.

Aadhya made room for his bag. She stayed at Ram's shoulder until he had taken her place.

"Seedha utha doon?" the watchman asked.

"Ruko. Pehle saans dekhne do."

Ram tried to turn towards Rudra. The medic kept him from twisting onto the injured side.

"Baat mat karo."

Rudra could hear each word. He could not take his eyes off the cable yet.

---

Dhruv's ring-hand was shaking. The old rope burns had opened where its edge pressed the palm. His thin line still kept the supporting block from sliding; he had not left it to help at the desk.

"Pehle bojh utaarenge," he said.

He pointed to the catch which kept the big wheel from returning.

"Tum wheel sambhaalo. Main yeh uthaaunga. Ek daant. Phir baitha doonga."

Rudra put both forearms against a broad spoke on the wheel's outer side, away from the teeth. The metal was thick enough to bear the pressure his hands could not measure finely.

He drove against it, backwards.

The bent coupling shifted a fraction in its jam. The catch loosened against its tooth.

Dhruv used the torn-off control handle as a short bar. Keeping his ring-hand at the supporting block, he lifted the catch with the bar's end.

"Ab. Dheere."

The cable took the wheel back against Rudra's arms.

It wanted to go faster than that.

He braced his left foot on the sound ledge and let his body move with the spoke. Gold crossed the darker pressure along his forearms. His wrists shook inside it. He could slow the return; he could not make it feel light.

Dhruv lowered the catch into the next tooth.

The wheel stopped against it.

Above the saddle, the thick sleeve had begun to sag.

They did it again.

This time a loose curve of cable touched the stone beneath the house wall.

No new plaster fell. The building did not straighten or mend. It simply stopped being pulled towards the iron.

"Bas," Dhruv said.

Rudra took his arms off the spoke.

The catch held. Dhruv set the broken control handle down, then eased his pale line away from the frame's foot. With the cable's pull gone, the block sat squarely under the load.

The outer frame stayed upright without a person holding it.

Rudra looked once at the house crack, following it up to the hinge Aadhya had stepped away from.

Empty. Damaged. Still standing.

He turned back to the ring.

---

"Mujhe hata do," Ira said to Veer.

He did not try to lift her. He named the stair edges while she moved back on her own hand and heels, one step at a time. Then he shifted along the terrace behind her, keeping the slung arm clear of the stone.

Vedant drew the remaining water down the empty service channel. Dhruv moved to the river-stair side and raised his angle-ring between the frame and the upper turning.

Aadhya helped the two watchmen take Ram's stretcher to the dry bend under the medic's direction. They carried the ends; she steadied the injured side with her forearm, not the two numb fingers. They had not reached the road, but the falling iron could no longer reach his chest.

"Neeche khaali," Dhruv called.

Rudra waited until Vedant had taken his basin clear of the frame's foot.

Then he put his left forearm against the fixed collar and the right against the short shaft beyond it.

The crack he had made was between them.

He pulled the two sides across each other.

Not the whole frame. Not the cable. That one broken joint.

Iron gave a low, tearing note.

The four struck beneath his breastbone. The longer allowance met it late; for a moment his arms tried to take opposite instructions. He could feel the outline thinning over his injured ankle, feel how much of him was already being held together by the act of staying upright.

He did not find another count.

He put his weight into the crack.

The shaft broke.

The inner ring dropped out of alignment.

Between the iron bands the darkness narrowed sharply, as if a curtain had been drawn edge-on. Smoke struck its edge and curled back towards the sky instead of going down.

Rudra caught the falling inner band below its broken mount, keeping his hands on iron, out of the black gap.

He turned it towards the empty service steps.

The band bent across the fractured collar. Bolts tore free. The remaining strip of darkness shivered between two pieces of metal which were no longer facing each other.

Then Rudra drove the bent band down.

It struck the stair in a shower of stone and split at the old mounting hole.

The opening folded to a line.

Closed.

A boat-chain knocked beyond it.

Through the broken frame Rudra could see a narrow flame on the far bank, doubled in ordinary moving water.

---

The water beside Rudra's foot lay flat.

He waited for it to rise again.

A leaf turned in the runnel and floated past the broken shaft. Beyond the iron, smoke drifted across the river. It did not fall into an unseen depth.

Nothing was open there now.

He took his hands off the inner band. It stayed on the empty steps where he had put it, bent through its mount, no longer joined to the split collar. The main lever was up. Its torn-off stop-plate lay on the empty step. The house cable rested loose beneath the saddle.

Behind the fallen desk a dark sleeve moved.

Rudra could see the empty hand feeling for the ledge. No handle remained within its reach. The man did not get back to his feet.

"Desk ke peechhe," Rudra told Dhruv. "Zinda hai."

Dhruv turned towards the river stair to call the watchmen down by the other side.

Rudra took a step towards his father.

The right ankle gave under him.

He sat hard on the sound ledge. The outline thinned along his shoulders, then over his arms. Red-black drew close to the skin and went with the next breath. The gold withdrew from the body-shape without taking the bleeding seam with it.

He had not decided exactly how to end it. He could not have kept it another moment simply because people were watching.

Without the form, the swollen ankle hurt even when he kept it still.

For several seconds he could only sit there and breathe.

Vedant came from the channel. Ordinary water continued down the drain after he put his basin aside. He opened the dressing packet the medic had left on top of his bag and pressed the clean pad against Rudra's neck seam.

"Haath yahan."

Rudra put his left hand over the pad. The knuckle cuts protested. Vedant kept his own hand there until the pressure was steady.

"Chal sakte ho?"

Rudra looked towards the bend.

"Chalna hai."

Vedant offered his shoulder and helped him stand. He kept the pace slow enough that the ankle did not fold again.

---

Someone at the road end had begun saying that the river had come back.

"Ganga kahin gayi nahi thi," a man answered. He was the householder, still carrying the unused length of his torn towel. "Darwaaza band hua."

A second voice was calling Rudra's name. A third asked which lane had the injured.

The words crossed each other above the stretchers. There would be a story later, much larger than the broken joint or the people who had braced it. For now the householder moved people out of the way and shouted for the middle of the road to be cleared.

Aadhya stayed beside the stretcher.

The medic had loosened Ram's outer shirt and was listening with a stethoscope at the right side of his chest. He shifted the instrument, listened again, then put two fingers to the wrist above the dirty bandage.

Ram's eyes were open. He had not taken his hand away from the ribs.

"Papa," Rudra said.

His father found him with his eyes.

The gaze went straight to the dressing against Rudra's neck, then to the blood down his left arm.

Even now. Even with that shallow breath.

"Band ho gaya," Rudra told him.

Ram's eyes moved beyond him towards the steps.

"Haan," Rudra said. "Sach mein."

A little tension left the hand against the ribs. Not the pain. The medic could see the difference too; he caught the wrist before Ram tried to push himself higher.

"Aap mat hilliye."

Ram's lips moved.

Rudra bent closer, letting Vedant take the neck dressing again.

"Tumhari maa?"

"Gaadi mein. Sitara saath hain."

It was the last thing Rudra knew, and he gave it without adding an arrival, a doctor's promise or a recovery he had not seen.

Ram shut his eyes for a moment.

When he opened them, they took longer to settle on Rudra's face.

---

The medic lifted one edge of the shirt farther from the right ribs. A dark bruise was spreading there above the iron-strike mark. Ram coughed once. A little blood marked the corner of his mouth.

He tried to lay two fingers beside the bruise. A little warmth gathered beneath them. Then the hand shook and fell back onto the cloth. His breathing did not ease.

Aadhya looked down at her own shaking hands and kept them away from the bruise.

The medic looked only at Ram now.

"Hospital ko bolo chest injury hai. Nabz kamzor. Ambulance kahan tak aayi?"

The watchman at the road called back that another vehicle had been requested. No light was at this turning yet.

"Main road tak le chalo. Ruko mat."

The two bearers lifted together. Aadhya kept pace on the injured side, her forearm ready if the shoulder shifted. Her right thumb and index finger stayed straight; the other fingers shook against the rail.

Rudra reached for the stretcher's upper handle.

His hand would not stay closed.

He let the bearer keep it. Put his own empty hand beside his father's instead.

Behind them the river remained visible through the broken frame. Nothing rose to follow. The wheel did not turn.

Ram's eyes closed again.

"Papa?"

The medic touched the wrist, then leaned close enough to feel the next breath.

"Saans hai," he said. "Chalte raho."

Rudra laid two fingers in the open, bandaged palm as they went towards the road.

"Is baar aap chhodna mat."

His father's hand did not close around them.

---

---
